Lecture 1B
Genetic Disorders
Dr. Neil Voss
Aug 28, 2023
What is a genetic disorder?
What is the difference between a disorder
and a disease?
Disease vs. Disorder
A disease is a pathophysiological response to internal or external factors. Have a characteristic set of signs and symptoms. Used as labels for ill health. Such as heart disease.
A disorder is a disruption to regular bodily structure and function. Can simply mean something is not working right. An irregular heartbeat is a disorder
Diseases may cause disorders, and disorders can lead to diseases.
What is a genetic disorder?
Genetic Disorder
A genetic disorder is a genetic problem caused by one or more abnormalities in the genome
Most genetic disorders are quite rare and affect one person in every several thousands or millions.
Genetic disorders can be:
monogenic or single-gene
polygenic or multifactoral (many genes)
chromosomal
In terms of genetic disorders, what types of anomalies can happen to your DNA?
Types of Gene Disorders
Point mutation
Deletion
Chromosome abnormalities:
Deletion
Duplication
Translocation
Inversion
Insertion

Genetics Disorder Activity
Do the following for each item:
Describe the disorder

What are the symptoms of the disorder?
Can it be inherited? Is it dominant or recessive?
At what age can the disorder be diagnosed? Pre-natal, at birth, childhood, adulthood
Are certain populations more likely to have the disorder?
What is the prognosis? How long do the affected patients live? 
Is there a treatment for the disorder?

Lecture 2B
Genes, Genomes, and Genetic Analysis
Chapter 1
Dr. Neil Voss
Sept 11, 2023
Define Genetics?
Genetics
the study of heredity and the variation of inherited characteristics.
study of genes, genetic variation, and heredity in organisms.
resemblances and differences of related organisms resulting from the interaction of their genes
scientific study of how genes control the characteristics of plants and animals
study of both trait and molecular inheritance mechanisms of genes
modern genetics has expanded beyond inheritance to studying the function and behavior of genes
What is genomics?
What is the different between genetics and genomics?
Genes, Genomes, and Genetic Analysis
Genetics is the study of heredity
and how genes control the inherited characteristics
Genomics is the study of all of the genes in an organism
the structure, function, evolution, and mapping of genomes
Genes are the elements of heredity
What are the differences between molecularbiology, biochemistry, cellbiology, and genetics?
Definitions of Biological Fields
Biochemistry is the study of the chemical substances and vital processes occurring in living organisms.
Biochemists focus heavily on the role, function, and structure of biomolecules. The study of the chemistry behind biological processes and the synthesis of biologically active molecules.
Genetics is the study of the effect of genetic differences on organisms.
Often this can be inferred by the absence of a normal component (e.g., one gene). It is the study of mutants with respect to the normal phenotype. Genetic interactions can often confound simple interpretations of such "knock-out" studies.
Molecular biology is the study of molecular underpinnings of the processes of replication, transcription, translation, and cell function.
The central dogma of molecular biology where genetic material is transcribed into RNA and then translated into protein. 
Cell biology is the study of cells, organelles, cell cycle, and cell division
their physiological properties, their structure, the organelles they contain, interactions with their environment, their life cycle, division and death. This is done both on a microscopic and molecular level.
Fundamental concepts
Each organism has its own GeneticBlueprint that makes it different from others.
The genetic information is stored in the chromosomes located in the nucleus.
The genetic information is stored as discrete instructions called genes.
History of Genetics
The existence of genes was first suggested by the work of Gregor Mendel in 1866
Classical genetics is the approach to the study of genetics through analysis of the offspring of matings.
Friedrich Meischer discovered nucleic acids in 1869.
Molecular genetics is the analysis of differences between species through the study of DNA itself.

Evidence for Chromosomes
Nuclei of male and female reproductive cells undergo fusion during fertilization
Chromosomes split and each daughter cell receives an identical set 
Chromosome number may differ between species but is the same within a particular species

The Idea of
the Gene
Mendels experiments with pea plants led him to postulate on the existence of heritable factors
In 1909, Archibald Gerrod suggested that genotype dictates phenotype through enzymes that catalyze specific chemical reactions
Early History
Metabolism as a cause of hereditary disease
Blocks in metabolic pathways can cause disease
Alkaptonuria  excretion of homogentisic acid
Phenylketonuria  phenylalanine accumulation
Linking genes and enzymes was helped by understanding that cells synthesize and degrade most organic molecules in a series of steps, a metabolic pathway.
Gene = Enzyme
Enzyme 1
Enzyme 2
Enzyme 3
Reaction 1
Reaction 3
Reaction 2
Starting
molecule
Product
George Beadle and Edward Tatum exposed bread mold to X-rays, creating mutants that were unable to survive on minimal medium as a result of inability to synthesize certain molecules
Using crosses, they identified three classes of arginine-deficient mutants, each lacking a different enzyme necessary for synthesizing arginine
They developed a one geneone enzyme hypothesis, which states that each gene dictates production of a specific enzyme

Enzyme A

Gene = Polypeptide
Many proteins are composed of several polypeptides, each of which has its own gene. Beadle and Tatums hypothesis is now: 
one geneone polypeptide
Note that it is common to refer to gene products as proteins rather than polypeptides
Which of the following contradicts the one-gene, one-enzyme hypothesis?
A) A mutation in a single gene can result in a defective protein.
B) Alkaptonuria results when individuals lack a single enzyme involved in the catalysis of homogentisic acid.
C) Sickle-cell anemia results in defective hemoglobin.
D) A single antibody gene can code for different related proteins, depending on the splicing that takes place post-transcriptionally.
Answer: D

The genetic code
What does it mean when we say the genetic code is redundant?
A) A single codon (three nucleotides) can specify the addition of more than one amino acid.
B) The genetic code is different for different domains of organisms.
C) The genetic code is universal (the same for all organisms).
D) More than one codon can specify the addition of the same amino acid.
Answer: D
Genetic Code
The genetic code is redundant and unambiguous
Redundant:
degenerate; being in excess; characterized by verbosity or unnecessary repetition
two different codons can specify the same amino acid
Unambiguous:
unequivocal; clear; indistinct; having only a single meaning or interpretation
no codon specifies more than one amino acid
codon
amino acid
Mutation
Mutations can lead toaltered proteins
The M1V mutant in the PAH gene.
The R408W mutant in the PAH gene.

Dr. Neil Voss

Today we are talking about DNA.
What are some various genome sizes...
Define Genome.
Genome
A genome is an organism's complete set of DNA
including all of its genes.
In humans, a copy of the entire genome is contained in all cells that have a nucleus.
In theory, each genome contains all of the information needed to build and maintain that organism.

C) 600 million
D) 6 billion
E) 60 billion

Human Genome Project
The goals of the Human Genome Project include
identifying all the genes in human DNA
determining the sequences of the more than 3 billion base pairs in human DNA
storing this information in databases
improving tools for data analysis
transferring related technologies to the private sector
addressing the ethical, legal, and social issues that would arise from this knowledge

Human Genome Project
One of the most interesting projects in modern genetics was the sequencing of the entire human genome
A genome is the complete set of genes (and alleles) in a certain organism
The Human Genome Project, begun in 1989 and essentially finished in 2003, was a massive research undertaking

Differences in Human DNA
There are service that looks at only the highly variable parts of Human DNA
Based on single nucleotide polymorphisms (SNPs or snips)
The nucleotide diversity between humans is about 0.1%
But only 1% of these are expected to have any meaningful difference
so only about 30,000 letters are important
The service checks certain letters in your DNA that are associated with diseases.
23andMe sequences about 650,000 SNPs.

Promethease
Promethease is a literature retrieval system that builds a personal DNA report based on connecting a file of DNA genotypes to the scientific findings cited in SNPedia.
Customers of DNA testing services (such as 23andMe, Ancestry.com, FamilyTreeDNA, etc.), can retrieve information published science articles about their DNA variations.
Number of Genes
Minimal Organism
Mycoplasma laboratorium is a designed, partially synthetic species of bacterium
It is estimated that the synthetic genome cost US$40 million to make and took 20 people more than a decade of work.
Version 3.0  contains 531,560 base pairs and 473 genes.
Of these genes 149 are of unknown biological function.
announced March 24, 2016

Summary
Complexity  # of Genes
More complex organisms do not always have larger genomes than simpler ones.
Any given genome seems to contain more DNA than would be needed for the predicted gene number
Some closely related species exhibit divergent DNA contents.

Complementary Sequences
1. Which one of the following sequences is complimentary to the sequence ATGC?
A. CGTA
B. CGAT
C. GCTA
D. TACG
E. ATGC
Answer: D
2. Which one of the following DNA sequences is complimentary to the direction-less DNA sequence CGC,ATA,GG?
A. GGA,TAC,GC
B. GCG,TTA,GG
C. CGC,AAT,CC
D. GCG,TAT,CC
E. GCG,TCC,TA
Answer: D
3. Which one of the following sequences is complimentary to the sequence 5'-ATGC-3'?
A. 5'-CGTA-3'
B. 5'-GCAT-3'
C. 5'-CGAT-3'
D. 5'-TACG-3'
E. 5'-ATGC-3'
Answer: B
3-TACG-5
4. Which one of the following sequences is complimentary to the sequence
3'-CAA,AGG,T-5'?
Hint: pay attention to the 5' and 3' directions!
A. 5'-GTT,TCC,A-3'
B. 5'-CAA,AGG,T-3'
C. 5'-ACC,TTT,G-3'
D. 5'-TGG,AAA,C-3'
E. 5'-TGG,TCC,A-3'
Answer: A
Base Composition
Base composition (%G+C) varies among organisms
[A] = [T]
[G] = [C]
[A] + [G] = [T] + [C] = 50%
[A] + [C] = [T] + [G] = 50%
These rules hold true regardless of the % G+C or the source of the DNA
Chargaff's Rules
A sample of dsDNA contains 14% Adenine, what percentages are the other nucleobases?
Chargaff's Rules
A sample of dsDNA contains 14% Adenine, what percentages are the other nucleobases?
[A] = 14%
[A] = [T]
so [T] = 14%
Chargaff's Rules
A sample of dsDNA contains 14% Adenine, what percentages are the other nucleobases?
[A] = [T] = 14%
100 14  14 = 72%
[G] + [C] = 72%; [G] = [C]
2 [G] = 72%;  [G] = 36%
[G] = [C] = 36%
Chargaff's Rules
A sample of dsDNA contains 14% Adenine, what percentages are the other nucleobases?
[A] = [T] = 14%
[G] = [C] = 36%

What is a palindrome?
Literary Palindromes
A palindrome is a word, phrase, number, or other sequence of characters which reads the same backward or forward.

Restriction Enzymes
Restriction enzymes cleave DNA at a specific sequence
Restriction enzymes
The DNA recognition sequence is usually a 4-6 bp palindrome
Restriction enzymes are named after the organisms from which they were isolated
The cuts may result in blunt or sticky ends
Sticky ends may have 5' or 3'-overhangs

Fragmenting DNA:
Gel Electrophoresis
One indirect method of rapidly analyzing and comparing genomes is gel electrophoresis
This technique uses a gel as a molecular sieve to separate nucleic acids or proteins by size
A current is applied that causes charged molecules to move through the gel
Molecules are sorted into bands by their size
Two Major Types
Agarose
gels are easily cast and handled compared to other matrices, because the gel setting is a physical rather than chemical change
Poly-acrylamide (PAGE)
Mixture of acrylamide and bis-acrylamide powder used to create a gel. 
Care must be used when creating this type of gel, as acrylamide is a potent neurotoxin in its liquid and powdered form.
Agarose Polymer
Agar melts at 85 C (185 F) and solidifies around 32-40 C (90-104 F). This property lends a suitable balance between easy melting and good gel stability at relatively high temperatures. Gel density is controlled by concentration of agarose
Negatively charged DNA moves toward the positive pole
Apparatus for gel electrophoresis.
Size separation of DNA fragmentsby electrophoresis in agarose gels
DNA molecules must pass through pores in the gel - smaller fragments migrate more rapidly
DNA migration distance in a gel is related to its size
DNA migration asfunction of base pairs
Fig. 20-9a
Mixture ofDNA mol-ecules ofdifferentsizes

The story of
Baby 81
Baby 81 incident
The Baby 81 incident was a widely reported parental identity dispute about a baby who survived the 2004 Indian Ocean earthquake.
The baby, around 2 months old, was reportedly found on the beach.
After remaining unclaimed for several days, nine couples were reported to have said that the child was theirs

Blood Typing
Human leukocyte antigen (HLA) typing
HLA Genes
Cell-surface proteins are responsible for the regulation of the immune system in humans.
HLA genes are highly polymorphic, which means that they have many different alleles.
They are the major cause of organ transplant rejections. 
HLA genes areclose together
cross-overis unlikely
Inheritance of HLA Haplotypes
In this example Child 2 and Child 4 have matching HLA genes and antigens.
Each parent passes one of their two haplotypes with a total of four possible combinations
Siblings have a 1 in 4 (25%) chance of being HLA identical 
Identical twins almost always have matching haplotypes, except in rare event where there is crossover between the HLA genes.

Summary
Baby 81
The 2 month old baby was found on the beach on 26 December 2004 the day of the earthquake 
On 14 February 2005, the results of the DNA tests confirmed the couple's claim
The family were permitted to take the child home on 16 February 2005, almost 2 months after the earthquake
It was later reported that only one couple had claimed the baby at the hospital, and that the press circus surrounding the baby was blamed for delaying his return to his family.

DNA Fingerprinting:
Overview
Types of DNA markers present in genomic DNA
Single nucleotide polymorphism (SNP)
Restriction fragment length polymorphism (RFLP)
Tandem repeat polymorphisms
simple sequence repeat (SSR)
variable number of tandem repeats (VNTR)
Copy number polymorphism (CNP)
Concepts in genetic localization of genetic risk factors for disease.
Disease genes can be mapped using DNA markers
Using Restriction Sites for Genetic Profiling
DNA Profiling
DNA profiling is a method used to identify an individual from a sample of DNA by looking at unique patterns in their DNA.
It is essentially a DNA fingerprint
Many different methods have been developed for DNA profiling individuals using DNA fragments and restriction enzymes.
Different aspects of our genome lead to the ability to fingerprint individuals
RFLPs, VNTRs, and STRs
A difference in the DNA sequence of two molecules can be detected if the difference eliminates a restriction site.
RFLPs are due to SNPs in restriction sites
RFLPs are codominant
RFLPs create DNA fragments of different sizes. 
The fragments are the same size in a homozygote.
The fragments are different sizes in a heterozygote.
In a restriction fragment length polymorphism (RFLP), alleles may differ in the presence or absence of a cleavage site in the DNA.
Tandem repeat polymorphism
A genetic polymorphism in which the alleles in a population differ in the number of copies of a DNA sequence 
(typically 260 bp) that is repeated in tandem along the chromosome.
Tandem repeat polymorphisms
Also called microsatellites
tract of repetitive DNA in which certain DNA motifs (213 base pairs in length) are repeated, typically 550 times.
abundant in the human genome
often highly polymorphic in human populations
VNTR
Variable number of tandem repeats
~ 10-60 nt
DNA Fingerprinting:
Short Tandem Repeats (STR)
An individuals unique DNA sequence, or genetic profile, can be obtained by analysis of tissue or body fluids 
DNA testing can identify individuals with a high degree of certainty
Genetic profiles are currently analyzed using genetic markers called short tandem repeats (STRs)

Boseman Science:
Whos the Daddy?
Whos the father?
Paternity Sample Rules
1. ALL of the bands in the child must come from a parent
every band in the child must be accounted for
2. Only some bands from each parent are present in the child.
Just because the mother/father has a band does not mean the child has to have it.
3. If the child has a band that the mother is missing, it must come from the father.
Whos the father?
1. ALL of the bands in the child must come from a parent
2. Only some bands from each parent are present in the child.
3. If the child has a band that the mother is missing it must come from the father.
Whos the father?
1. ALL of the bands in the child must come from a parent
2. Only some bands from each parent are present in the child.
3. If the child has a band that the mother is missing it must come from the father.
see red circles
Answer: A
Paternity Sample Rules

every band in the child must be accounted for

Crime Scene
Investigation
(CSI)
Answer: A
Crime Scene Sample Rules
1. ALL of the bands in the crime scene sample must come the victim or the criminal
every band in the crime scene sample must be accounted for
2. ALL bands from both the criminal and victim are present in the crime scene sample
If the suspect has a band that is not in the crime scene sample, then they must be innocent
3. If a band from the crime scene is not present in the victim, then the criminal must have that band.
Which suspect was at the crime scene?

crime scene sample, then they must be innocent
Which suspect was at the crime scene?
every band in the crime scene sample must be accounted for

DNA Fingerprinting:
Single Nucleotide Polymorphisms
SNP Genotyping
SNP genotyping is the measurement of genetic variations of single nucleotide polymorphisms (SNPs).
It is a form of genotyping, which is the measurement of more general genetic variation.
A single-nucleotide polymorphism (pronounced snips )
is a substitution of a single nucleotide at a specific position in the genome, that is present in a sufficiently large fraction of the population (e.g. 1% or more)
Size of Different Genotypes
Human Diploid Genome
6 billions base pairs
Human Exome
part of the genome composed of exons, the sequences which become proteins
30 million base pairs
Single Nucleotide Polymorphisms (SNPs)
roughly 4 to 5 million SNPs (0.1-0.2%) in each person's genome compared to the reference human genome
There are service that looks at only the highly variable parts of Human DNA
Based on single nucleotide polymorphisms (SNPs or snips)
The nucleotide diversity between humans is about 0.1%
But only 1% of these are expected to have any meaningful difference
so only about 30,000 letters are important
The service checks certain letters in your DNA that are associated with diseases.
23andMe tests 640,000 SNPs
Differences in Human DNA
Oligonucleotides attached to a glass slide in a SNP chip can be used to identify duplex DNA molecules containing alternative base pairs for a SNP, in this example a TA base pair versus a CG base pair.
SNP chips are used to identify SNPs
The SNP genotype of an individual can be determined by hybridization because DNA from genotypes that are homozygous TA/TA, homozygous CG/CG, or heterozygous TA/CG each gives a different pattern of fluorescence.

Answers on Race
As human populations spread around the globe, with people living in relative isolation for millenniums, some differences emerged. 
Belief in genetic causes of racial inequality remains widespread in the United States
Health disparities between black and white Americans are more readily explained by environmental effects such as racism than the DNA they inherited from ancestors
However, variation between members of the same race is often greater than variation between races.
Answers on Sex/Gender
there is more genetic variation within human sexes than there is between them
it is hard to categorize a person as male or female based just on their brain
females/girls and males/boys are much more similar to each other than students, and perhaps teachers, realize
Most Textbooks are Wrong
The Humane Genetics project explores the role biology education can play in tackling this problem. 
When biology education causes students to perceive too much genetic variation between racial groups, it can increase racial prejudice.
Will this curricular intervention help students and teachers believe that males/boys are not innately better at STEM than females/girls? 
Could interventions like this play a role in reducing sex/gender disparities in STEM?

In this webinar, Dr. Brian Donovan shares why Genetics education needs to move beyond Mendel to combat white supremacy.
Eugenics
Our day-to-day conversations tend to overestimate the differences between humans, whether they pertain to skin pigmentation, culture, linguistics, personality, etc. 
While such differences can be enriching, misinterpretation of their importance can be outright dangerous and clearly not based in any sort of mathematical logic.
However, variation between members of the same race is often greater than variation between races.

The Eugenics Crusade (2017) 
The Eugenics Crusade tells the story of the eugenics movement and its long history in the United States, from its beginnings in the study of heredity, to its rise as a popular movement promising to uplift the human race through state sponsored sterilization, to its influence on immigration laws designed to close our borders to groups deemed genetically inferior.

Promethease
Promethease is a literature retrieval system that builds a personal DNA report based on connecting a file of DNA genotypes to the scientific findings cited in SNPedia.
Customers of DNA testing services (such as 23andMe, Ancestry.com, FamilyTreeDNA, etc.), can retrieve information published science articles about their DNA variations.

23andme has just shown that I've been accidentally dating my half-sister

Christmas day rolls around, and Sarah had bought the both of us 23andme DNA kits. The thing is, is that we were both conceived by in-vitro fertilization via sperm donors. The Golden State Killer
Golden State Killer DNA 
1. used DNA taken from an old crime scene
2. upload DNA to public genealogy database [GEDmatch]
3. find distant relatives with matching genetic profiles
matches found in GEDmatch were to relatives of the suspect, not the suspect himself.
site provides family trees, able to look for relatives who did not upload genetic data
4. family profile was created, and find feasible "suspects" within a family.
5. eliminate suspects based on age, sex, and places of residence
6. prove that his DNA matched that from the crime scenes
7. lifted trace of DNA from suspect car door while at Hobby Lobby
8. arrest and convict

Blood Typing
via SNP Genotype
23andMe Paper
We classified haplotypes into blood groups on the basis of genotypes at three SNPs:
rs8176747, rs41302905, and rs8176719.
A deletion at rs8176719 confers a type O haplotype
as does a T allele at rs41302905. 
If neither rs8176719 nor rs41302905 confers type O, 
then rs8176747 distinguishes between types A and B.
The blood group assignment methodology described is incomplete
there are other rare variants that can influence blood group.
compared to self-reported blood groups from over 1.47 million research participants, 
the genetic assignments achieved 90-94% precision and 72-96% recall depending on blood group.
Trans-ethnic analysis reveals genetic and non-genetic associations with COVID-19 susceptibility and severity.
SNPedia rs8176719
rs8176719 represents a site in the ABO gene, often referred to as either c.261delG or, less commonly, c.260_262insG, and it is a key SNP in determining blood group type O status. [PMID 2333095]
An allele that encodes either a blood group type A or type B will have a (G) at this SNP site, i.e. such an allele will be rs8176719(G). 
If a one-base deletion has occurred at this site, removing this nucleotide all together, the corresponding allele is considered to be rs8176719(-) and encodes the most common blood group type O allele. 
However, an individual will typically only be blood group type O if they are carry two copies of (are homozygous) for this deletion, in other words, if their genotype is rs8176719(-;-). 
If they carry one copy, they could be blood type A, or blood type B (but are unlikely to be type O).
A person who is rs8176719(G;G) is likely to be of blood type A, B, or AB,
but due to the existence of (rare) nonfunctional O alleles brought about by (nondeletional) mutations at other positions than rs8176719, they could at least theoretically still be phenotyped as type O.
i4001527 or rs590787 are relevant to Rh+ or Rh-
Spreadsheet
Dr. Voss likely type O
Actually, Im A+

The Human Genome Project
Officially begun as the Human Genome Project in 1990, the sequencing of the human genome was largely completed by 2003
The genome was completed using sequencing machines and the dideoxy-chain termination method
A major thrust of the project was development of technology for faster sequencing
<number>
J. Craig Venter
Two approaches complemented each other in obtaining the complete sequence
The initial approach built on an earlier storehouse of human genetic information
Then J. Craig Venter set up a company to sequence the entire genome using an alternative whole-genome shotgun approach 
This used cloning and sequencing of fragments of randomly cut DNA followed by assembly into a single continuous sequence

Whole Genome Sequencing
The cost to generate a high-quality 'draft' whole human genome sequence in mid-2015 was just above $4,000;
by late in 2015, that figure had fallen below $1,500.
The cost to generate a whole-exome sequence was generally below $1,000. 
Commercial prices for whole-genome and whole-exome sequences have often (but not always) been slightly below these numbers.
What is the difference between exome and genome?

Lecture 4B
Gregor Mendel
Chapter 3
Dr. Neil Voss
Sept 25, 2023
Gregor Mendel
Born in 1822 in a country now called the Czech Republic
Gregor grew up in a village with 71 houses, 479 people, 41 horses, and 98 cows. His father was a farmer.
Growing up his village learned the benefits of selective cross-breeding to create better apples and sheep with thicker wool.
These initial ideas were said to have inspired Gregor.
School Years
Gregors parents scraped together the funds to send to the local boarding school.
But they did not have enough money to pay for all of his meals.
At age 16, Gregors father was injured and could no longer earn money for Gregors education.
School Years
Gregor began working as a tutor and paid his own way through four more years of school.
The hardship of paying for his studies, caused Gregor to become a friar at the Abbey of St. Thomas.
Friar Mendel
Friars preached, cared for the sick, taught school, and were community leaders.
They were also mathematicians, botanists, philosophers, and geologists
They had a library with 30,000 books and had several great thinkers to discuss ideas
Gregor Mendel (third from right, holding a sprig of fuchsia in the back) with his fellow monks.
Figure 14.1a What principles of inheritance did Gregor Mendel discover by breeding garden pea plants? (part 1: Mendel with fellow monks)
<number>
University of Vienna
The abbot of the Abbey, reward Gregors thirst for knowledge by sending him to the University of Vienna.
There Gregor learned about the laws of nature and how to study nature using carefully planned experiments.
Mendel the Teacher
When Gregor returned to the abbey after finishing the university, he taught science at a nearby school.
Though he liked teaching, Gregor wanted to make a great discovery himself.
He was fascinated by one of the hottest topics of the time:
How do mothers and fathers  animals and plants  pass down traits to their children? 
Mendel the Researcher
Gregor read how earlier scientists had paired different species of plants to see what their offspring would be like.
The offspring were called hybrids.
Many hybrids (corn, wheat, flowers) were created by farmers, but only a few were ever studied.
Gregors Plan
Mendel could control the process of fertilization, 
To understand the processes of inheritance
Breed thousands of offspring from just a few pairings.
Count how often specific traits appeared.
See if the statistics would help him find a pattern.
Choice of Plant
He studied 34 kinds of peas in his garden.
Such a wide variety it was hard for Gregor to decide which traits to study.
He looked closely and first noticed the color of the seeds could be either green or yellow.
Further some were smooth and some were wrinkled. 
Mendel Identified seven characters of pea plants for his study.
A heritable feature that varies among individuals (such as flower color) is called a character
Each variant for a character, such as purple or white color for flowers, is called a trait
Mendels Experimental, Quantitative Approach
Seven Characters of Mendel
Table 14.1a The results of Mendels F1 crosses for seven characters in pea plants (part 1)
<number>
Table 14.1b The results of Mendels F1 crosses for seven characters in pea plants (part 2)

Traits and Characters
Character  an observable feature of a particular group of organisms, such as flower color
Trait  each variant for a character, such as purple or white color for flowers
Each character is associated with multiple traits.
Other advantages of using pea plants
Short generation time, about five weeks
Large numbers of offspring, about 120 seeds per plant
individual plants have both male and female reproductive organs
Mating is easily controlled
plants could be allowed to self-pollinate or could be cross pollinated
Display binary traits
Mendel chose to track only those characters that occurred in two distinct alternative forms
Controlled Fertilization
True-Breeding Plants
He also used varieties that were true-breeding
plants that produce offspring of the same variety when they self-pollinate
Gregor wanted to be sure changes in traits were due to the experiment.
Gregor spent two years preparing a set of parental plants that were true-breeding!!!
Then he began his experiments to create hybrids...
When Gregor mated true-breeding yellow and green plants  it produced all yellow.
For all seven characters, all of the hybrid children looked like just one of the parents.
Were the traits lost forever?
What will their grandchildren and great-grandchildren look like?
Gregor pondered these questions throughout a snowy winter.
Come spring, Gregor planted the seeds of the hybrids.
At harvest time, he discovered green and yellow peas sitting side by side in the same pod.
The missing traits reappeared.
Gregor concluded that every plant has two building blocks to create any one traits.
One dominant and one recessive
The mother and father, each give one block to their child.
Today, we understand the molecular mechanisms that cause this.
In 1865, Gregor Mendel presented his findings and a year later published a scientific paper.
No one at the time understood his discovery.
They thought it only applied to peas
Mendel went on to become abbot and no longer had time for experiments.
Mendel died in 1884.
Mendels Discovery
Mendels legacy
In 1900, 16 years after his death, three different scientists in three different countries re-discovered his 1866 paper.
They were trying to repeat his discovery and were stunned by the brilliance and foresight of Mendel.
Mendels extraordinary experiments and insights brought us the terms dominant and recessive 
Mendel didnt know about the existence of genes or chromosomes or the details of cell division
Gregor Mendel is known as the first geneticist and his discoveries are called Mendels laws.
Mendel was not alone in his quest to understand inheritance
what set him apart was a combination of extensive research and studious note-taking
precise field notes continued over a period of many years
His approach allowed him to deduce principles that had remained elusive to others
Mendels legacy
Summary
Traits and Characters
Character  an observable feature of a particular group of organisms, such as flower color
Trait  each variant for a character, such as purple or white color for flowers
Each character is associated with multiple traits.

Lecture 4E
Mendelian Genetics
First Two Principles
Chapter 3
Dr. Neil Voss
Sept 25, 2023
Mendels FourPrinciples of Heredity
(1) Principle of Paired Factors
Each organism has two alleles that make up the genotype for a given trait
(2) Principle of Dominance
In each genotype there is a dominant allele, the phenotype is determined by dominant allele
(3) Law of Segregation or Law of Purity of Gametes
In sexual reproduction, each parent contributes just one of their alleles to its offspring
(4) Law of Independent Assortment
different pairs of alleles are passed onto the offspring independently of each other.
inheritance of genes at one location does not influence the inheritance of genes at another location.

Key concepts and terms
Genotype
Phenotype
Homologous chromosomes
Locus

Key concepts and terms
Genotype - the genetic constitution of an organism
Phenotype - the observable traits of an organism
Homologous chromosomes - in a diploid organism, the two copies of a chromosome inherited from the mother and the father
Locus - location of a gene on a chromosome
Allele - different versions of the same gene
Homozygous - the two copies of a gene are identical
Heterozygous - the two copies of a gene are different
Phenotype
Phenotypes
Phenotype is the set of observable characteristics of an individual resulting from the interaction of its genotype with the environment.
Results from BOTH the expression of genetic code AND the influence of environmental factors.
Phenotype is all the characteristics displayed by an organism
which includes morphology, biochemistry, behavior, development, physiological, and molecular.
What are the different types of phenotypes?
morphology, molecular, etc.
Phenotypes
Morphological phenotype
Morphology is shape and structure.
Molecular phenotyping involves determining phenotype at the molecular level.
Useful to determine genotype 
Molecular Phenotyping
The wildtype allele W is dominant to the mutant allele w, which contains an insertion of foreign DNA.
Figure 3.2: (A) W (round) is an allele of a gene that specifies the amino acid sequence of starch branching enzyme I (SBEI). (B) w (wrinkled) is an allele that encodes an inactive form of the enzyme because its DNA sequence is interrupted by the insertion of a transposable element. (C) At the level of the morphological phenotype, W is dominant to w: Genotypes WW and Ww have round seeds, whereas genotype ww has wrinkled seeds.

Key Features of Single Gene Inheritance
Genes come in pairs so each cell has two alleles of a gene
For each pair of genes, the alleles may be the same or different
Each gamete (sperm or egg) contains only one allele of each gene
Each organism has two alleles that make up the genotype for a given trait
For the population on the right there are two alleles
(A and a).
Each circle has two of the alleles
they can be both the same or one of each

What principles account for the passing of traits from parents to offspring?
How do traits of parents combine to produce offspring?
What principles account for the passing of traits from parents to offspring?
Two hypotheses:
blending hypothesis
particulate hypothesis
Drawing from theDeck of Genes
<number>
Blending hypothesis
The blending hypothesis is the idea that genetic material from the two parents blends together
like blue and yellow paint blend to make green
<number>
Figure 14.UN11 Test your understanding, question 18 (shirt phenotypes)
<number>
Blending hypothesis
The blending hypothesis is the idea that genetic material from the two parents blends together
like blue and yellow paint blend to make green
If this process were true, the variation in a population would be reduced
a fact that caused Charles Darwin and many other early biologists to doubt the validity of the hypothesis. 
<number>
Particulate Hypothesis
The particulate hypothesis is the idea that parents pass on discrete heritable units (genes)
Mendel documented a particulate mechanism through his experiments with garden peas
Mendel came up with the idea of dominance
<number>
Dominance

different pairs of alleles are passed onto the offspring independently of each other.
inheritance of genes at one location does not influence the inheritance of genes at another location.
Dominance
In each genotype there is a dominant allele, the phenotype is determined by dominant allele
Since purple is more common than white, purple is likely dominant to white

A is the dominant allele
a is the recessive allele
Homozygous Dominant - the two identical copies of the dominant allele
Homozygous Recessive - the two identical copies of the recessive allele
Heterozygous  one of each allele, one dominant and one recessive

Cross Experiments
Chapter 3

Terminology for Cross Experiments
P: parental generation
F1: offspring of P
F2: offspring of F1
F3: offspring of F2
F4: ...
True Breeding
Garden peas allow for self-pollination as well.
Mendel was able to determine if a plant was true-breeding by self-pollinating the plant and looking at its offspring.
True-breeding plants always will produce offspring of the same phenotype.
Hybrid Plants
In order to study traits, Mendel conducted cross experiments.
Garden peas allow for self-pollination as well.
Mendel produced hybrid plants using controlled cross-pollination of true-breeding parents
Crossing true-breeding plants that differ in a trait produces a hybrid.
An organism with two identical alleles for a character is homozygous for the gene controlling that character
true-breeding organisms are homozygous
An organism that has two different alleles for a gene is heterozygous for the gene controlling that character
heterozygotes are hybrids and not true-breeding
Zygosity Vocabulary
The Monohybrid Cross
A monohybrid cross is a mating between individuals who have different alleles at one genetic locus of interest.
A cross between two individuals that are both heterozygous for one character is called a monohybrid cross

Hybrid Cross Terminology
P: parental generation
true-breeding, i.e., homozygous for all genes
mates have different phenotype, e.g., purple and white flowers
F1: offspring of P
heterozygous for all genes
all display dominant phenotype, purple
F2: offspring of F1
exhibit interesting ratios of phenotypes
e.g., 3:1 ratio in monohybrid cross
F3: offspring of F2
F4: ...
Hybrid Crosses
A cross between two individuals that are both heterozygous for one character is called a monohybrid cross
Aa  Aa
A cross between two individuals that are both heterozygous for two characters is called a dihybrid cross
AaBb  AaBb
A cross between two individuals that are both heterozygous for three characters is called a trihybrid cross
AaBbCc  AaBbCc
Reciprocal Crosses
Pea plants are sexual organisms
A cross can be preformed in two ways:
Male purple x Female white
Male white x Female purple
For Mendel, the outcome of reciprocal cross did not depend on which trait was in the male or the female:
A reciprocal cross should show no difference, unless the gene is sex-linked (Chapter 5)
Testcross
A cross between a heterozygote (or unknown) and a homozygous recessive is a testcross
In a testcross of a Ww heterozygous parent with a ww homozygous recessive, the progeny are Ww and ww in the ratio of 1 : 1.
Testcross
The phenotypes of the progeny reveal the relative frequencies of the different gametes produced by the heterozygous parent
The recessive parent contributes only recessive alleles.
Mendels testcrosses
Types of Crosses
Testcross: breeding the mystery individual with a homozygous recessive individual
Hybrid crosses:
A cross between two individuals that are both heterozygous for one character is called a monohybrid cross
A cross between two individuals that are both heterozygous for two characters is called a dihybrid cross
Three characters is a trihybrid cross, etc.
Backcross: hybrid organisms (F1) are crossed with one of the parental (P) genotypes
Reciprocal cross: swapping the traits of the male and female in a cross.

Lecture 4G
Mendelian Genetics
Law of Segregation
Chapter 3
Dr. Neil Voss
Sept 25, 2023
Mendels FourPrinciples of Heredity
(1) Principles of Paired Factors
Each organism has two alleles that make up the genotype for a given trait
(2) Principle of Dominance
In each genotype there is a dominant allele, the phenotype is determined by dominant allele
(3) Law of Segregation or Law of Purity of Gametes
In sexual reproduction, each parent contributes just one of their alleles to its offspring
(4) Law of Independent Assortment
different pairs of alleles are passed onto the offspring independently of each other.
inheritance of genes at one location does not influence the inheritance of genes at another location.
Mendels Observation
The Unexpected Phenotypes of the F2 Generation 
When Mendel crossed contrasting, true-breeding white- and purple-flowered pea plants, all of the F1 hybrids were purple
When Mendel crossed the F1 hybrids, many of the F2 plants had purple flowers, but some had white
Mendel discovered a ratio of about three to one, purple to white flowers, in the F2 generation
Observations from monohybrid crosses
F1 hybrids express the dominant phenotype
In the F2, plants with the recessive trait unexpectedly reappeared
In the F2, the ratio of the dominant phenotype to the recessive phenotype is 3:1
Very consistent
Mendel Tried This for Seven Different Characters
Mendel developed a hypothesis to explain the 3:1 inheritance pattern he observed in F2 offspring
Four related concepts make up this model
These concepts can be related to what we now know about genes and chromosomes 
Mendels Model
Mendel's Law
of Segregation
Law of Segregation
The Law Most Relates to Gamete Formation
A Gamete is a mature haploid male or female germ cell which is able to unite with another of the opposite sex in sexual reproduction to form a zygote.
haploid gametes
The Law of Segregation
In the formation of gametes, the paired hereditary determinants separate (segregate) in such a way that each gamete is equally likely to contain either member of the pair.
Law of Segregation
(1) Genes come in pairs
(2) For each pair of genes, alleles can be same or different
(3) Gametes contain only one allele
(4) Gametes are equally likely to contain each allele
(5) Union of gametes is a random process

What are the

Law of Segregation
(1) Alleles come in pairs
(2) For each pair of alleles, they can be same or different
(3) Gametes contain only one allele
(4) Gametes are equally likely to contain each allele
(5) Union of gametes is a random process
Mendels Law of Segregation predicts how a single characteristic associated with a single gene is inherited.
In some cases, though, we might want to predict the inheritance of two characteristics associated with two different genes.
How can we do this?
Hypothesis of Dependent Assortment
Prediction ofDependent Assortment
Dependent assortment predicts the alleles are always linked:
F and the S alleles are linked i.e. purple flower always goes with yellow seed, and 
f and the s alleles are linked i.e. white flower always goes with green seed.
only certain combinations are possible: FS and fs
When a dihybrid cross is conducted, dependent assortment predicts offspring with a phenotypic ratio of:
3 purple-flower/yellow-seed:
1 white-flower/green-seed.
Prediction of
Independent Assortment
Independent assortment predicts the alleles are not linked. 
All combinations are possible: FS, Fs, fS, and fs. 
When a dihybrid cross is conducted, independent assortment predicts a phenotypic ratio of:
9 purple-flower/yellow-seed
3 purple-flower/green-seed
3 white-flower/yellow-seed
1 white-flower/green-seed

Mendel's Law of

Mendels FourPrinciples of Heredity
(1) Principles of Paired Factors
Each organism has two alleles that make up the genotype for a given trait
(2) Principle of Dominance
In each genotype there is a dominant allele, the phenotype is determined by dominant allele
(3) Law of Segregation or Law of Purity of Gametes
In sexual reproduction, each parent contributes just one of their alleles to its offspring
(4) Law of Independent Assortment
different pairs of alleles are passed onto the offspring independently of each other.
inheritance of genes at one location does not influence the inheritance of genes at another location.
Mendel's 2nd Law
The Law of Independent Assortment
Segregation of the members of any pair of alleles is independent of the segregation of other pairs in the formation of reproductive cells.
Law ofIndependent Assortment
Alleles at one locus segregate into gametes independently of alleles at other loci.
Not valid for single genes problems
not valid for blood types
not valid for AA x Aa
Requires at least two genes to apply, e.g. genes A and B.
dihybrid (or higher) cross
Wrinkled/round does not affect yellow/green

Dihybrid cross
Using a dihybrid cross, Mendel developed the law of independent assortment
It states that each pair of alleles segregates independently of each other pair of alleles during gamete formation
This law applies only to multiple genes on different, non-homologous chromosomes or those far apart on the same chromosome
Genes located near each other on the same chromosome tend to be inherited together
The coat color gene and seed shape genes assort (segregate) independently.
3 : 1 ratio of round : wrinkled
3 : 1 ratio of yellow : green
produces
9 : 3 : 3 : 1 ratio observed in the F2 progeny of the dihybrid cross.
Results of a dihybrid cross
Genotypes and phenotypes of the F2 progeny of the dihybrid cross for seed shape and seed color.
Genotype and phenotype ratios in the F2 of a dihybrid cross

The multiplication rule states that the probability that two or more independent events will occur together is the product of their individual probabilities
Probability in an F1 monohybrid cross can be determined using the multiplication rule 
Segregation in a heterozygous plant is like flipping a coin: Each gamete has a  chance of carrying the dominant allele and a  chance of carrying the recessive allele
Multiplication Rule
Answer
Proportion for each gene is:
A:  , B:  , C:  , D:  
A, B, C, and D are independent, so multiplication rule applies:
 x  x  x  = 1/16

Assuming complete dominance and independent assortment.
George Aa Bb Cc dd Ee ff Gg and 
Linda AA bb cc dd ee Ff Gg
are crossed answer the following questions.
(a) How many different gametes does George produce?
(b) How many different gametes does Linda produce?
(c) How big would a Punnett square for this cross be?
(d) How many possible genotypes are created in this cross?
(e) How many possible phenotypes are possible from this cross?
Method
1. build a table
2. do each gene one at a time.
George: both gametes, A or a; = 2
Linda: only A gametes; = 1
George: A or a
Linda: A
Cross: AA or Aa; = 2
Phenotype: both dominant; = 1
now you do gene B
B or b  2
b  1
Bb or bb  2
2 phenotypes
now finish the table
1 x 1  1
2 x 1  2
2 x 2  3!
what about phenotypes?
Note: dominant or recessive homozygotes give different phenotype counts
how do we merge the genes together?
these genes are independent, so we multiply
Punnett square is just the number of gametes multiplied: 32 x 4
Punnett square is just the number of gametes multiplied: 32 x 4 = 128
Assuming complete dominance and independent assortment. George Aa Bb Cc dd Ee ff Gg and Linda AA bb cc dd ee Ff Gg are crossed answer the following questions.
(a) How many different gametes does George produce? 
32gametes
(b) How many different gametes does Linda produce?
4gametes
(c) How big would a Punnett square for this cross be?
32x4
(d) How many possible genotypes are created in this cross? 
96genotypes
(e) How many possible phenotypes are possible from this cross? 
32phenotypes
Note: the number of phenotypes is always less than or equal to the number of genotypes
phenotypes <= genotypes

Unknown Alleles
Hartl Question 3.23
Based on their coat colors of the parent alone, we can predict that genotypes of:
Female = solid tan = aa S
Male = solid black = A S
dashes are used to say that we dont know the second allele
A means AA or Aa
Look at genotypes of offspring:
2 solid tan: Saa
2 solid black: SA
1 spotted tan: ssaa
1 spotted black: ssA
Female = solid tan = aa S
Male = solid black = A S
Because of tan offspring (aa) is produced, the male must also have one recessive allele for gene A.
Female = aa S
Male = Aa S
Since there are recessive spotted offspring (ss), both parents must have one recessive allele for gene S.
Female = aa Ss
Male = Aa Ss

Lecture 5C
Pedigrees
Chapter 3
Dr. Neil Voss
Oct 2, 2023
Pedigree Charts
Symbols
Mating Lines
Pedigree
Symbols used in a pedigree diagram
Rare Dominant Diseases
Pedigree showing the inheritance of a dominant disease gene
The diseased individuals are present in every generation (indicates a dominant disease).
Males and females are both about equally affected (indicates autosomal inheritance)
Characteristics of rare dominant alleles
1. Females and males are equally likely to be affected.
2. Affected offspring have one affected parent and the affected parent is equally likely to be the mother or father.
3. On average, half of the individuals in sibships with an affected parent are affected.
Rare Recessive Disorders
Inheritance of an autosomal recessive disease gene
The heterozygous individuals are phenotypically wild type. 
Mating of two heterozygotes is required to produce an affected (homozygous recessive) child.
Characteristics of rare recessive alleles
1. Females and males are equally likely to be affected.
2. Affected individuals, if they reproduce, usually have unaffected progeny.
3. Most affected individuals have unaffected parents.
4. The parents of affected individuals are often relatives.
5. Among siblings of affected individuals, the proportion affected is approximately 25%.
Comparison of Dominant vs. Recessive (ignore sex-linked)
Dominant:
2. Affected offspring have one affected parent
3. On average, half (50%) of the individuals in sibships with an affected parent are affected.
Recessive:
2. Affected individuals have unaffected progeny.
3. Most affected individuals have unaffected parents.
4. The parents of affected individuals are often relatives.
5. Among siblings of affected individuals, the proportion affected is approximately 25%.
There is no masking of one allele by another allele for DNA markers (codominance of alleles is seen)
A human pedigree showing segregation of SSRP alleles
X-linked Disorder
Pedigree
(Chapter 4 Preview)
X-Linked Recessive
Well cover X-linked disorders in Chapter 4.
in recessive males (squares) are more likely to be affected than females (circles).
X-linked Recessive
in X-linked recessive males (squares) are more likely to be affected than females (circles).
X-linked Recessive
in X-linked recessive males (squares) are more likely to be affected than females (circles).
X-linked Dominant
in X-linked dominant females (circles) are more likely to be affected than males (squares).
X-linked Dominant
in X-linked dominant females (circles) are more likely to be affected than males (squares).
Y-linked
in Y-linked males (squares) are the only ones that can be affected. 
Females (circles) have no Y chromosome.

How do we do questions like this???
Probability speak...
union/or
intersection/and
mutually exclusive/disjoint
independent
Probability speak...
Union: probability that either of two events will occur
P(A or B)
Intersection: probability that two events occur at the same time
P(A and B)
Mutually Exclusive: Two events are mutually exclusive if they cannot occur at the same time. Another word that means mutually exclusive is disjoint. 
intersection: P(A and B) = 0
Addition Rules
Addition Rule
The addition rule states that the probability that any one of two or more exclusive events will occur is calculated by adding together their individual probabilities
The rule of addition can be used to figure out the probability that an F2 plant from a monohybrid cross will be heterozygous rather than homozygous
Probability speak...
Specific Addition Rule: If two events are mutually exclusive, then the probability of either occurring is the sum of the probabilities of each occurring.
P(A or B) = P(A) + P(B)
General Addition Rule: In events which aren't mutually exclusive, there is some overlap.
When P(A) and P(B) are added, the probability of the intersection (and) is added twice.
To compensate for that double addition, the intersection needs to be subtracted. 
P(A or B) = P(A) + P(B)  P(A and B)
Probability speak...
In the OR case the overlap is counted twice so we must subtract it.
P(A or B) =
P(A) + P(B) 
 P(A and B)

Multiplication Rules
The multiplication rule states that the probability that two or more independent events will occur together is the product of their individual probabilities
P(A and B) = P(A)  P(B)
Probability in an F1 monohybrid cross can be determined using the multiplication rule 
Segregation in a heterozygous plant is like flipping a coin: Each gamete has a  chance of carrying the dominant allele and a  chance of carrying the recessive allele
Special Multiplication Rule
General Multiplication Rule
The probability that Events A and B both occur is equal to the probability that Event A occurs times the probability that Event B occurs, given that A has occurred, P(B|A).
P(A and B) = P(A)  P(B|A) = P(B)  P(A|B) 
P(A|B) = The probability that Event A occurs, given that Event B has occurred, is called a conditional probability.
If A and B are independent, then:
P(A and B) = P(A)  P(B)
Conditional Probability
The conditional probability of Event B, given Event A has happened, is denoted by the symbol P(B|A).
If A and B are independent then P(B|A) = P(B)
Conversion:
P(B|A) = P(AandB)/P(A)
P(A|B) = P(AandB)/P(B)
Bayes' Theorem
Part (A)
Because the disease is rare, assume father heterozygous
50% chance son received bad allele
HDhd
Part (A)
Because it is rare, assume father heterozygous
50% chance son received bad allele
Part (B)
A: chance that young man had bad allele
B: chance that child will get bad allele
P(A and B) = P(B|A)  P(A)
P(B|A) = 50%
P(A) = 50%
P(A and B) = 25%
Summary
Multiplication Rules
Specific Multiplication Rule, if two events, A and B, are independent, then:
P(A and B) = P(A)  P(B)
General Multiplication Rule, if two events, A and B, are NOT independent then:
P(A and B) = P(A)  P(B|A) = P(B)  P(A|B)
Addition Rules
Specific Addition Rule: if two events, A and B, are mutually exclusive, then:
P(A or B) = P(A) + P(B)
General Addition Rule: if two events, A and B, are NOT mutually exclusive, then:
P(A or B) = P(A) + P(B)  P(A and B)

Ab gamete

50% x 0% = 0%
50% x 50% = 25%
Addition rule:
25% + 0% = 25% chance of Ab gamete
AB gamete
50% AABB  100% chance of AB
50% AABb  50% chance of AB
Multiplication rule:
50% x 100% = 50%
50% x 50% = 25%
Addition rule:
25% + 50% = 75% chance of AB gamete
25% chance of Ab gamete
75% chance of AB gamete
Can they produce other gametes?
Good, because 25% + 75% = 100%!

Lecture 5F
Degrees of Dominance
Chapter 3
Dr. Neil Voss
Oct 2, 2023
Inheritance of characters by a single gene may deviate from simple Mendelian patterns in the following situations:
When alleles are not completely dominant or recessive
When a gene has more than two alleles
When a gene produces multiple phenotypes
Extending Mendelian Genetics for a Single Gene
Complete dominance occurs when phenotypes of the heterozygote and dominant homozygote are identical
In incomplete dominance, the phenotype of F1 hybrids is somewhere between the phenotypes of the two parental varieties
In codominance, two dominant alleles affect the phenotype in separate, distinguishable ways
In epistasis commonly is the suppression of the effect of one gene by another.
Degrees of Dominance
Incomplete Dominance
the phenotype of F1 hybrids is somewhere between the phenotypes of the two parental varieties

Polygenic Inheritance
two or more genes affect the phenotypic expression of a single trait
Polygenic Inheritance
Monogenic inheritance refers to genetic control of a phenotype or trait by a single gene. 
Digenic inheritance refers to the interaction of only two genes that affect the phenotypic expression of a single trait.
Many traits are not determined so simply, and either involve the interaction of many genes
Multifactorial inheritance results from the interaction of many genes and with environmental effects.
Polygenic inheritance results from the interaction of many genes, but has no environmental effects. 
Polygenic Inheritance
Polygenic inheritance refers to a single inherited phenotypic trait that is controlled by two or more different genes.
polygenic traits may display a range of possible phenotypes, determined by a number of different genes and the interactions between them.
Examples of Polygenic Inheritance

Bateson and Punnett
In 1909, Bateson and Punnett decided two cross two varieties of pea, each of which was pure-breeding for white flower
This cross yielded an F1 generation in which all progeny had purple flowers. 
Next, two F1 plants were crossed to create the F2 generation.
In this generation, Bateson and Punnett counted a total of 382 purple-flowered plants and 269 white-flowered plants.
The ratio of purple flowers to white flowers was thus 9.4:6.6, or approximately 9:7.

Bateson & Punnett 1909

<number>
Bateson and Punnett
In 1909, Bateson and Punnett decided two cross two varieties of pea, each of which was pure-breeding for white flower
For the F1 generation, all progeny had purple flowers. 
For the F2 generation, they found a ratio of purple flowers to white flowers of approximately 9:7.
Bateson proposed an ability of an "allelomorphic pair" (pair of gene alleles) to mask the affects of the alleles for another gene.
It seemed that two recessive alleles at one flower locus could mask the effects of the alleles at the other flower locus.
Bateson and Punnett
Let's designate the first locus as the C locus, and the second as the P locus.
If Bateson's theory held true, it meant that any flower with the cc genotype would be white, no matter what alleles were present at its P locus.
Similarly, any flower with the pp genotype would also be white, no matter what alleles were present at its C locus.
Bateson later used the word "epistasis," which translates as "standing upon," to define the masking action of one gene by another.
Bateson and Punnett
Many years after Bateson first described this 9:7 phenotypic ratio in pea plants, researchers were finally able to determine the two genes responsible for it (Dooner et al., 1991).
These genes control flower color by controlling pea plant biochemistry, in particular that related to pigment compounds called anthocyanins.
In peas, there is a two-step chemical reaction that forms anthocyanins; gene C is responsible for the first step, and gene P is responsible for the second.
If either step is nonfunctional, then no purple pigment is produced, and the affected pea plant bears only white flowers.
The dominant C and P alleles code for functional steps in anthocyanin production, whereas the recessive c and p alleles code for nonfunctional steps. Thus, if two recessive alleles occur for either gene, white flowers will result.
What are the Phenotypes?
The dominant C and P alleles code for functional steps in anthocyanin production, whereas the recessive c and p alleles code for nonfunctional steps.
Thus, if two recessive alleles occur for either gene, white flowers will result.

Thus, if two recessive alleles occur for either gene, white flowers will result.

Thus, if two recessive alleles occur for either gene, white flowers will result.

In 9:7 epistasis, the A_ bb, aa B_ and aa bb genotypes all have the same phenotype.
What are the genotype ratios in a test-cross?
Test-cross with a double heterozygote has a 1:1:1:1 genotype ratio.
Since the final three genotypes map to one phenotype, a test cross of a F1 from a dihybrid cross will be

Chromosome Complements
Somatic cells have a fixed number of chromosomes
Chromosomes exist in pairs for diploid organisms
Gametes have one copy and are haploid
Diploid chromosome numbers for some plants and animals
The Cell Cycle
The cell cycle consists of
Mitotic (M) phase (mitosis and cytokinesis)
Interphase (cell growth and copying of chromosomes in preparation for cell division)
Interphase is about 90% of the cell cycle

Which of the following statements about mitosis is FALSE?
A) Mitosis is cell division that produces two daughter cells.
B) Each daughter cell formed by mitosis has the half the number of chromosomes as the parent cell.
C) Each daughter cell formed by mitosis has the same kinds of chromosomes as the parent cell.
D) The parent cell and the daughter cells are genetically identical.
Answer: B
Mitosis has four stages
Chromosome number does not change
Two daughter cells are produced in the end

Meiosis
Chapter 4
Dr. Neil Voss

Meiosis
Diploid to Haploid
Two rounds of cell division
Sexual Life cycle
Meiosis produces haploid daughter cells
In meiosis, there are two successive nuclear divisions but only one round of DNA replication
First nuclear division:
homologous chromosomes are separated from each other 
each daughter chromosome consists of two identical chromatids
Second nuclear division:
resembles mitotic division
the sister chromatids separate and four haploid daughter cells are produced.

Meiosis Prophase I Terminology
Homologous  not an exact copy but having the same structural features and pattern of genes
Homologous chromosomes  chromosome pairs (one from each parent) that are similar in length, gene position, and centromere location
Synapsis  process of pairing of homologous (maternal and paternal) chromosomes
Sister chromatids  two identical copies of a single replicated chromosome. They are joined at the centromere
Crossing over  the exchange of genes between homologous chromosomes, resulting in a mixture of parental characteristics in offspring
Chiasmata  the point of contact (or physical link) between two homologous chromosomes where crossing over occurs
Prophase I isincredibly complex(more than Prophase II)and is divided intosix (6) sub-stages
Stages of Prophase I
1. Leptotene
chromosomes start to condense 
chromosomes first become visible
thread-like chromomeres are seen
2. Zygotene
synapsis begins
the pairing of homologous chromosomes begins
the synapsis process forms numerous points of contact
Stages of Prophase I
Pachytene 
Crossing over of genetic material occurs between homologous chromosomes
3. Early Pachytene
synapsis (pairing) is complete
crossing over begins at the chiasmata
4. Late Pachytene
chromosomes continue to shorten and thicken
sister chromatids first become visible
Homologous chromosomes can undergo extensive crossing over
Interpretive drawing of a bivalent consisting of a pair of homologous chromosomes.
Figure 13.9b
Crossover
Chiasmata
Holliday junction
Figure 13.9b Crossing over and synapsis in prophase I: a closer look (part 2) 
<number>
Stages of Prophase I
5. Diplotene
chiasma are fully visible at this stage
homologous chromosomes begin to migrate apart
held together only by the chiasmata
6. Diakinesis
chromosomes are at their most condensed form
homologous chromosomes reach maximum repulsion 
before separation in anaphase I
WARNING!

have overlapping
definitions in

Sub-Stages of Prophase I
Prophase I
1. Leptotene  chromosomes first become visible
2. Zygotene  synapsis or homologous chromosome pairing begins
3. Early Pachytene  pairing is complete
4. Late Pachytene  sister chromatids become visible
5. Diplotene  chromosomes become shorter and thicker; chiasmata are prominent
6. Diakinesis  homologous chromosomes repel; they are held together by chiasmata

*** After telophase I of meiosis, after the first cell division, the chromosomal makeup of each daughter cell is _____.
A) diploid, and the chromosomes are each composed of a single chromatid
B) diploid, and the chromosomes are each composed of two chromatids
C) haploid, and the chromosomes are each composed of a single chromatid

Answer: D
Matter of Clarification
Haploid  a single set of chromosomes
Diploid  two complete sets of chromosomes, one from each parent
Later when we discuss polyploids (week 11 or so), we will distinguish:
monoploid  basic set of chromosomes that is multiplied in a polyploid
haploid  chromosomes present in a gamete, independent of total chromosome number  usually half the chromosome number
For diploids:
monoploid = haploid

Independent Assortment produces many different combinations
Drosophila, fruit flys, have 4 chromosomes
so you have two choices for each chromosome (red or blue)
which results in 2 to the 4th power combinations
24 = 16 combinations.
Humans have 23 pairs chromosomes
so you have two choices for each chromosome
which results in 2 to the 23rd power combinations
223 = 8,388,608 combinations
Independent Assortment produces many different combinations
Chickens have 39 pairs of chromosomes
239 = 550 billion combinations.
The Adder's tongue fern has 630 pairs of chromosomes
2630 = 4.5 x 10189 combinations
That is 4.5 duosexagintillion
Bread wheat is hexaploid and has six sets of its 7chromosomes
67 = 279,936 combinations

Comparison of Mitosis and Meiosis
Mitosis conserves the number of chromosome sets, producing cells that are genetically identical to the parent cell
Meiosis reduces the number of chromosomes sets from two (diploid) to one (haploid), producing cells that differ genetically from each other and from the parent cell

The diploid number of a roundworm species is 4. Assuming there is no crossover, and random segregation of homologues during meiosis, how many different possible combinations of chromosomes might there be in the offspring (not including variety generated by crossing over)?
A) 4
B) 8
C) 16
D) 64
Answer: C
The diploid number of a roundworm species is 4. Assuming there is no crossover, and random segregation of homologues during meiosis, how many different possible combinations of chromosomes might there be in the offspring (not including variety generated by crossing over)?
A) 4 possible gametes (no fertilization)
B) 8
C) 16 possible offspring (= 4 x 4; after fertilization)
D) 64
Answer: C

Lecture 6E
X-Linked Genes Part 1
Chapter 4
Dr. Neil Voss
Oct 9, 2023
Mendels Four Principlesof Heredity
(1) Principles of Paired Factors
Each organism has two alleles that make up the genotype for a given trait
(2) Principle of Dominance
In each genotype there is a dominant allele, the phenotype is determined by dominant allele
(3) Law of Segregation or Law of Purity of Gametes
In sexual reproduction, each parent contributes just one of their alleles to its offspring
(4) Law of Independent Assortment
different pairs of alleles are passed onto the offspring independently of each other. 
inheritance of genes at one location in a genome does not influence the inheritance of genes at another location.
Mendels Principleswere Imperfect
In Chapters 4 and5, we will be discussing the contributions of Thomas Hunt Morgan.
Morgan brought Mendels ideas to molecular biology 
in doing so Morgan expanded on and falsified Mendel original ideas.
Mendels Four Principlesof Heredity
(1) Principles of Paired Factors
Each organism has two alleles that make up the genotype for a given trait
NOT ALL ORGANISMS ARE DIPLOID
(2) Principle of Dominance
In each genotype there is a dominant allele, the phenotype is determined by dominant allele
CODOMINANCE, INCOMPLETE DOMINANCE and EPISTATIS
(3) Law of Segregation or Law of Purity of Gametes
In sexual reproduction, each parent contributes just one of their alleles to its offspring
(4) Law of Independent Assortment
different pairs of alleles are passed onto the offspring independently of each other. 
inheritance of genes at one location in a genome does not influence the inheritance of genes at another location.
The first solid evidence associating a specific gene with a specific chromosome came in the early 20th century from the work of Thomas Hunt Morgan
These early experiments provided convincing evidence that the chromosomes are the location of Mendels heritable factors
Morgan showed that inheritance has a physical basis
Morgans Choice of Organism
For his work, Morgan chose to study Drosophila melanogaster, a common species of fruit fly
Several characteristics make fruit flies a convenient organism for genetic studies
They produce many offspring, females lay about 400 eggs
A new generation can be bred every two weeks
They have only four pairs of chromosomes
<number>
Drosophila chromosomes
Wild Type
Morgan noted wild type, or normal, phenotypes that were common in the fly populations
wild type literally means the most common phenotype 
Traits alternative to the wild type are called mutant phenotypes
<number>
Wild-type Drosophila flies have red eyes (left). 
Among his flies, Morgan discovered a mutant male with white eyes (right).
This variation made it possible for Morgan to trace a gene for eye color to a specific chromosome (LMs).
<number>
Figure 15.3 Morgans first mutant
Correlating Behavior of a Genes Alleles with Behavior of a Chromosome Pair
In one experiment, Morgan mated male flies with white eyes (mutant) with female flies with red eyes (wild type)
The F1 generation all had red eyes
The F2 generation showed a 3:1 red to white eye ratio, but only males had white eyes
Morgan determined that the white-eyed mutant allele must be located on the X chromosome
Morgans finding supported the chromosome theory of inheritance

Basis of Sex
Males are more often affected by X-linked traits than females because _____.
A) male hormones such as testosterone often alter the effects of mutations on the X chromosome
B) female hormones such as estrogen often compensate for the effects of mutations on the X chromosome
C) X chromosomes in males generally have more mutations than X chromosomes in females
D) males are have only one X chromosome
Answer: D
In humans and other mammals, there are two varieties of sex chromosomes: a larger X chromosome and a smaller Y chromosome
A person with two X chromosomes develops as a female
while a male develops from a zygote with one X and one Y
Only the ends of the Y chromosome have regions that are homologous with corresponding regions of the X chromosome

In grasshoppers, cockroaches, and some other insects, there is only one type of sex chromosome, the X. 
Females are XX;
males have only one sex chromosome (X0). 
Sex of the offspring is determined by the sperm cell and whether it contains an X chromosome or no sex chromosome.
X-O System
<number>
Figure 15.6a Some chromosomal systems of sex determination (part 1: X-Y and X-0 systems)
(c) The Z-W system
76 +ZW
76 +ZZ
Z-W System
In birds, some fishes, and some insects, the sex chromosomes present in the egg (not the sperm) determine the sex of offspring. 
The sex chromosomes are designated Z and W. Females are ZW and males are ZZ.
<number>
Figure 15.6b Some chromosomal systems of sex determination (part 2: Z-W and haplo-diploid systems)
Z-linked inheritance in chickens
Females are heterogametic (WZ) 
Males are homogametic (ZZ)
Barred feathers in chickens, a classic example showing that chromosomal sex determination in birds is the reverse of that in mammals.
(d) The haplo-diploid system
32(Diploid)
16(Haploid)
Haplo-Diploid System
There are no sex chromosomes in most species of bees and ants. 
Females develop from fertilized eggs and are thus diploid. 
Males develop from unfertilized eggs and are haploid; they have no fathers.

How is sex determined in humans?
A) a gene present on the X chromosome that triggers female development
B) an autosomal gene that is required for the expression of genes on the Y chromosome 
C) a gene region present on the Y chromosome that triggers male development
D) an autosomal gene that is required for the expression of genes on the X chromosome
Answer: C
X-linked Genes
A gene that is located on either sex chromosome is called a sex-linked gene
Genes on the Y chromosome are called Y-linked genes; there are few of these
Genes on the X chromosome are called X-linked genes
X chromosomes have genes for many characters unrelated to sex
Whereas most Y-linked genes are related to sex determination
X genes and Y genes
<number>
X-linked Recessive
X-linked genes follow specific patterns of inheritance
For a recessive X-linked trait to be expressed
A female needs two copies of the allele (homozygous)
A male needs only one copy of the allele (hemizygous)
X-linked recessive disorders are much more common in males than in females
<number>
X-linked Recessive Disorders
Some disorders caused by recessive alleles on the X chromosome in humans
Color blindness (mostly X-linked)
Duchenne muscular dystrophy
Hemophilia
<number>
Color Blindness
Characteristics carried on the X chromosome include color blindness and hemophilia
the gene for color discrimination is on the X chromosome but not the Y
If a female XX carries the gene for color blindness on only one of her two X chromosomes
she will not express the defect, but half of her eggs will carry the defective gene
however, her sons have a 50-50 chance of being color-blind
Because the fertilizing sperm carries a Y chromosome, it cannot provide a second copy of that allele to correct the defect with a wildtype copy of the gene, resulting in a colorblind male child 
Color Blind Tests
Individuals lacking a light-absorbing photoreceptor for the color green will see a colorful fruit stand (left) as mostly yellows (right).
Can a color-blind male (XbY) and non-carrier wild-type vision female (XNXN) have a color-blind son?
Answer: No, the father always passes the Y chromosome
Sex-linked Punnett Square
A Punnett square forX-linked inheritance
each son gets his X chromosome from his mother and his Y chromosome from his father.

Red-green color blindness is a X-linked recessive trait in humans. 
Two people with wild-type color vision (N) have a color-blind son (b). 
What are the genotypes of the parents?
A) XNXN and XNY
B) XNXb and XNY
C) XbXb and XbY
D) XNXb and XbY
Answer: B
Duchenne muscular dystrophy is a serious condition caused by a recessive allele of a gene on the human X chromosome.
The patients have muscles that weaken over time because they have absent or decreased dystrophin, a muscle protein.
They rarely live past their twenties.
How likely is it for a woman to have this condition?
A) Women can NEVER have this condition.
B) One-fourth (25%) of the daughters of an affected man would have this condition.
C) One-half (50%) of the daughters of an affected father and a carrier mother could have this condition. 
D) Only if a woman is XXX (three X chromosomes) could she have this condition.
Answer: C

Summary
Mendels Four Principlesof Heredity
(1) Principles of Paired Factors
Each organism has two alleles that make up the genotype for a given trait
NOT ALL ORGANISMS ARE DIPLOID
X-LINKED GENES ARE HAPLOID for MALES
(2) Principle of Dominance
In each genotype there is a dominant allele, the phenotype is determined by dominant allele
CODOMINANCE, INCOMPLETE DOMINANCE and EPISTASIS
(3) Law of Segregation or Law of Purity of Gametes
In sexual reproduction, each parent contributes just one of their alleles to its offspring
MALES CONTRIBUTE EITHER ONE OR NO ALLELE IN MAMMALS
(4) Law of Independent Assortment
different pairs of alleles are passed onto the offspring independently of each other. 
inheritance of genes at one location in a genome does not influence the inheritance of genes at another location.

Probability of Progeny
Chapter 4
Dr. Neil Voss

Probability in predictions of progeny distribution
Genetic ratios result from:
chance assortment of genes into gametes
chance combination of gametes into zygotes 
Probability calculations in genetics frequently use the addition and multiplication rules of probability together
Equally Likely
Combinations
Assuming equal numbers of boys and girls.
If a mating has already produced a girl, what is the probability that the next child will be a boy?
A. 0, no chance
B. , 25% chance
C. , 50% chance
D. , 75% chance
E. 1, 100% chance
F. slightly higher than 50%, like 53%
The answer to both questions is 1/2. 
The probability argument is that each birth is independent of all the previous ones, so the number of boys or girls already born has no influence on the sexes of future children. 
How do we do this?
Probability that it is a boy = 0.5
Probability that it is a girl = 0.5
Probability of 7 boys = 0.57 = 0.0078125
Probability of 7 girls = 0.57 = 0.0078125
Probability of 4 boys and 3 girls = 0.57 = 0.0078125
Before we can do this problem we need to learn some math...
Factorials
Factorial
The factorial is the product of an integer and all the integers before it
factorial four, written as 4! is equal to:
4! = 1234 = 24.
The number gets really big, really fast
Short hand sometimes be used in the form:
n! = n  (n-1)!
4! = 4  3! = 4  6 = 24
Factorial
The factorial is the product of an integer and all the integers before it
factorial four, written as 4! is equal to:

Numberphile 0!
Why is 0 factorial equal to 1?
Why does 0! = 1
Binomial Distribution
In statistics, the binomial distribution give the probability of the number of successes in a sequence of n independent yes/no experiments, each of which yields success with probability p.
Binomial coefficient
is often read aloud as "n choose k", because there are ways to choose k elements, disregarding their order, from a set of n elements. 
Evaluating Binomial Coefficients
How many different ways can we get 4 boys and 3 girls?
Re-write how many different ways can we have 4 boys in 7 children
How many different ways can we get 4 boys and 3 girls? 35 ways!!!
How many different ways can we get 4 boys and 3 girls?
35 ways out of 128 (27) possible
 35/128 = 0.2734375  27.3%
4 girls and 3 boys the same 27.34%, these are mutually exclusive so
4 boys and 3 girls or 4 girls and 3 boys is
27.3% + 27.3%  54.7%
How many different ways can we get 4 boys and 3 girls?
Re-write how many different ways can we have 4 boys in 7 children
(a) 1/26 = 1/64 = 1.6%
(b) 6/64 = 9.4%
(c) 15/64 = 23.4%
(d) 20/64 = 31.3%
(e) (20+15+6+1)/64 = 42/64 = 65.6%
even numbers:
(1+28+70+28+1)/28 = 128/256 = 50%
use the choose function
a1. homozygotes:
there are 6 alleles and we choose 1 = 6possibilities
a2. heterozygotes
there are 6 alleles and we choose 2 different ones = 15 possibilities
(a) 15 + 6 = 21 possibilities for autosomal alleles
b1. female homozygotes:
there are 6 alleles and we choose 1 = 6possibilities
b2. female heterozygotes
there are 6 alleles and we choose 2 different ones = 15 possibilities
b3. male hemizygotes
there are 6 alleles and we choose 1 = 6possibilities
There are 21 possible genotypes for an autosomal gene:
6 homozygous and 15 heterozygous.
For an X-linked gene there are 27 possible genotypes.
6 homozygous and 15 heterozygous in females
6 hemizygous genotypes in males
