Lecture 8B
Chi-Square Method
Chapter 4
Dr. Neil Voss
Oct 30, 2023
Chi-Square (2) Test
Compares observed and expected data sets.
Expected data set was predicted by the null hypothesis
Measures the difference between observed and expected data.
Evaluates the "goodness of fit" between observed and expected.
If the difference is too large to be random, there's statistically significant evidence against the hypothesis.
Chi-Square (2) Terminology
Null Hypothesis (H0):
What we aim to find evidence against (want to reject).
Always simpler to calculate the expected numbers.
Alternative Hypothesis (Ha): Aim to support indirectly through the test.
Significance Level (): A preset threshold for statistical significance, usually set at 0.05.
The p-value: The probability of getting the observed data if the null hypothesis is true.
Chi-Squared statistic: The calculated measure that quantifies the difference between observed and expected data.
Critical Value: A threshold for comparison with the Chi-Squared statistic. 
The Critical Value depends BOTH on the Degrees of Freedom and Significance Level (). 
The Critical Value is obtained by looking at the Chi-Square (2) Distribution Table.
Degrees of Freedom: The number of independent variables that can vary in an analysis.
Null hypothesis: Reject or Accept?
If the null hypothesis is rejected, then we accept the alternative hypothesis.
Example: Null hypothesis: our situation is unbiasedAlternative hypothesis our situation is biased
Chi-squared statistic vs. Critical value:
Larger Chi-squared statistic values mean rejecting the null hypothesis.
Accept null hypothesis if Chi-squared statistic is less than Critical value.
Reject null hypothesis if Chi-squared statistic is greater than Critical value.
Example: Chi-squared statistic of 21.4 > Critical value of 7.82  Reject H0.
Critical value depends BOTH on the Degrees of Freedom and Significance Level ().
p-value vs. significance level ():
A larger Chi-squared statistic correlates with a smaller p-value.
Reject null hypothesis if p-value less than significance level ().
Accept null hypothesis if p-value greater than significance level () (fail to reject).
Example: p-value of 0.02 < fixed significance level () of 0.05  Reject H0.
Seven Steps of theChi-Square (2) Test
1. State Hypothesis
choose one that is easy to predict progeny
2. Predict expected progeny from hypothesis 
3. Subtract expected from observed (o-e)
square the value and then divide by expected
4. Sum each row to get the Chi Square statistic (2) value
5. Determine degrees of freedom (DF) 
6. Look up p-value using #4 and #5
7. Determine if hypothesis is rejected.

Figure 15.UN03b Skills exercise: using the chi-square test (part 2)
Chi-Square (2) Test
The 2 value means nothing on its own.
It is used to find the probability that the observed data set could have resulted from random fluctuations.
A low probability suggests that the observed data are not consistent with the hypothesis and the thus the hypothesis should be rejected.
A standard cutoff point used by biologists is a probability of 0.05 (5%). If the probability corresponding to the 2 value is 0.05 or less, the differences between observed and expected values are considered statistically significant and the hypothesis should be rejected.
Likewise if the probability is above 0.05, the results are not statistically significant; the observed data are consistent with the hypothesis.
df = 3
2 = Sum
0.178
As the chi-squared statistic of 0.178 is less than critical value of 7.82, we accept the null hypothesis.
Why we use a table?
It is quite a difficult function to calculate the exact p-value from the chi-square number, 2, and the degrees of freedom, k.

Seven Steps of theChi-Square (2) Test
1. State Hypothesis
choose one that is easy to predict progeny
2. Predict expected progeny from hypothesis 
3. Subtract expected from observed (o-e)
square the value and then divide by expected
4. Sum the result to get the 2 value
5. Determine degrees of freedom (DF) 
6. Look up p-value using #4 and #5
7. Determine if hypothesis is rejected.
df = 3
2 = Sum
6.76
As the chi-squared statistic of 6.76 is less than critical value of 7.82, we must accept the null hypothesis.

Are Mendel's numbers

Summary
Chi-Square (2) Test
Compares observed and expected data sets.
Expected data set was predicted by the null hypothesis
Measures the difference between observed and expected data.
Evaluates the "goodness of fit" between observed and expected.
If the difference is too large to be random, there's statistically significant evidence against the hypothesis.
Chi-Square (2) Terminology
Null Hypothesis (H0):
What we aim to find evidence against (want to reject).
Always simpler to calculate the expected numbers.
Alternative Hypothesis (Ha): Aim to support indirectly through the test.
Significance Level (): A preset threshold for statistical significance, usually set at 0.05.
The p-value: The probability of getting the observed data if the null hypothesis is true.
Chi-Squared statistic: The calculated measure that quantifies the difference between observed and expected data.
Critical Value: A threshold for comparison with the Chi-Squared statistic. 
The Critical Value depends BOTH on the Degrees of Freedom and Significance Level (). 
The Critical Value is obtained by looking at the Chi-Square (2) Distribution Table.
Degrees of Freedom: The number of independent variables that can vary in an analysis.
Null hypothesis: Reject or Accept?
If the null hypothesis is rejected, then we accept the alternative hypothesis.
Example: Null hypothesis: our situation is unbiasedAlternative hypothesis our situation is biased
Chi-squared statistic vs. Critical value:
Larger Chi-squared statistic values mean rejecting the null hypothesis.
Accept null hypothesis if Chi-squared statistic is less than Critical value.
Reject null hypothesis if Chi-squared statistic is greater than Critical value.
Example: Chi-squared statistic of 21.4 > Critical value of 7.82  Reject H0.
Critical value depends BOTH on the Degrees of Freedom and Significance Level ().
p-value vs. significance level ():
A larger Chi-squared statistic correlates with a smaller p-value.
Reject null hypothesis if p-value less than significance level ().
Accept null hypothesis if p-value greater than significance level () (fail to reject).
Example: p-value of 0.02 < fixed significance level () of 0.05  Reject H0.

6. Look up p-value using #4 and #5
7. Determine if hypothesis is rejected.

Lecture 8C
Linked Genes
Chapter 5
Dr. Neil Voss

Mendels Four Principles of Heredity
(1) Principles of Paired Factors
Each organism has two alleles that make up the genotype for a given trait
NOT ALL ORGANISMS ARE DIPLOID
SEX-LINKED GENES ARE HAPLOID for MALES
(2) Principle of Dominance
In each genotype there is a dominant allele, the phenotype is determined by dominant allele
CODOMINANCE, INCOMPLETE DOMINANCE and EPISTASIS
(3) Law of Segregation or Law of Purity of Gametes
In sexual reproduction, each parent contributes just one of their alleles to its offspring
MALES CONTRIBUTE EITHER ONE OR NO ALLELE IN MAMMALS
(4) Law of Independent Assortment
different pairs of alleles are passed onto the offspring independently of each other. 
inheritance of genes at one location in a genome does not influence the inheritance of genes at another location.
Mendel's Law of
Independent Assortment
Segregation of the members of any pair of alleles is independent of the segregation of other pairs in the formation of reproductive cells.
Alleles at one locus segregate into gametes independently of alleles at other loci.
Requires a dihybrid (or higher) cross
Wrinkled/round does not affect yellow/green

All F1 plants produceyellow-round seeds (YyRr).
LAW OFSEGREGATIONThe two alleles for each gene separate.
LAW OF INDEPENDENTASSORTMENT Alleles of genes on non-homologouschromosomes assortindependently.

Four kinds of gametes are produced in equal proportions
Alleles of two genes segregate independently
double homozygous individuals only produce one type of gamete 
A testcross can be used to show two genes are unlinked
Linked Genes
Genes on different chromosomes assort independently
A double heterozygote produces four possible types of gametes in equal proportions
But what if genes
are on the
same chromosome?
Linked Genes
Each chromosome has hundreds or thousands of genes (except the Y chromosome)
Genes located on the same chromosome that tend to be inherited together are called linked genes
Alleles of linked genes can be in cis or trans configuration
cis isomer: The two Xs are on the same side.
trans isomer: The two Xs are on opposite sides.
Cis-Trans Isomers
Geometric isomers are compounds with the same covalent partnerships that differ in their spatial arrangement around a carbon-carbon double bond.
The double bond does not allow atoms to rotate freely around the bond axis.
Figure 4.7b Three types of isomers, compounds with the same molecular formula but different structures (part 2: cis-trans isomers)

When you have two different alleles for two genes on the same chromosome, we need more specific nomenclature
You could have the recessive alleles for both genes on the same chromosome, or they could be split

Conclusion
Since most offspring had a parental (P generation) phenotype, Morgan concluded that the genes for body color and wing size are genetically linked on the same chromosome. 
However, the production of a relatively small number of offspring with non-parental phenotypes indicated that some mechanism occasionally breaks the linkage between specific alleles of genes on the same chromosome.
Recombination of Linked Genes: Crossing Over
Morgan discovered that genes can be linked, but the linkage was incomplete, because some recombinant phenotypes were observed
He proposed that some process must occasionally break the physical connection between genes on the same chromosome
That mechanism was the crossing over of homologous chromosomes
Crossing over
Takes place in prophase in meiosis I after chromosomes have duplicated
Results in reciprocal exchange of equal and corresponding segments of homologous chromosomes
Sites of crossing over are random along the length of chromosome pairs
Homologous chromosomes can undergo extensive crossing over

(A) Alleles in the same chromosome remain together when there is no crossing over between them.
(B) When there is crossing over, the result is two recombinant and two non-recombinant products, because the exchange is between only two of the four chromatids.
Crossing-over produces recombinant gametes

Parental type - allele configuration is the same as the parents in a cross
Recombinant type  non-parental type - allele combinations different from those in the parental chromosomes
Syntenic - refers to genes in the same chromosome regardless of whether they show independent assortment or linkage

Lecture 8D
Gene Mapping
Chapter 5
Dr. Neil Voss
Oct 30, 2023
Genetic Mapping
Gene Mapping describes the methods used to identify the locus of a gene and the distances between genes.
There are two distinctive types of "maps" used in the field of genome mapping: genetic maps and physical maps.
Genetic maps determine gene distances based on the genetic linkage information in offspring.
Physical maps are based on genome sequences and given in base pairs.
While the physical map are more "accurate", genetic maps often offer insights into the nature of different regions of the chromosome
the genetic distance to physical distance ratio varies greatly at different genomic regions which reflects different recombination rates, and such rate is often indicative of euchromatic (usually gene-rich) vs heterochromatic (usually gene poor) regions of the genome.
Concepts in genetic linkage
Parental type - allele configuration of the parents in a cross
Recombinant type - allele combinations different from those in the parental chromosomes
Frequency of recombination - percent value for recombinant chromosomes
Syntenic - refers to genes in the same chromosome regardless of whether they show independent assortment or linkage
Alleles of linked genes can be in cis or trans configuration
Recombination occurs at the same frequency whether the alleles are in cis or trans
In any genetic cross involving linked genes, the two most frequent types of gametes with respect to any pair of genes are parental type.
The two reciprocal products that result from any crossover or combination of crossovers (recombinant types) are expected to appear in approximately equal frequencies among the progeny.
Two General Principles
Linked or Unlinked
Genes with recombination frequencies <50% are linked and are on the same chromosome
Genes that have a recombination frequency of 50% are unlinked
these genes may be on non-homologous chromosomes or are located far apart in the same chromosome
Mapping the Distance between Genes
Recombination Frequencies
Linkage Map Distance
A linkage map is a genetic map of a chromosome based on recombination frequencies
Distances between genes can be expressed as map units;
one map unit, or centimorgan, represents a 1% recombination frequency
Map units indicate relative distance and order, not precise locations of genes
<number>

2 cM
Genetic distance between two genes can be represented four ways
Alfred Sturtevant, one of Morgans students, constructed a genetic map, an ordered list of the genetic loci along a particular chromosome
Sturtevant predicted that the farther apart two genes are, the higher the probability that a crossover will occur between them and therefore the higher the recombination frequency

One map unit represents 2% cross-over frequency

Figure 15.11 Research method: constructing a linkage map
Recombination Frequencies do NOT add!?!
The bvg recombination frequency (17%) is slightly less than the sum of the bcn and cnvg frequencies (9 + 9.5 = 18.5%).
This is because of the few times that crossover occurs between both bcn and cnvg.
A second crossover would cancel out the first, reducing the observed bvg recombination frequency.
The value of 18.5% (18.5 map units) is closer to the actual distance between the genes. 
In practice, a geneticist would add the smaller distances in constructing a map.

Genes on the same chromo-some whose alleles are soclose together that they donot assort independently(such as a, b, and c) are saidto be genetically linked.

Far Away Genes
Genes that are far apart on the same chromosome can have a recombination frequency near 50%
Such genes are physically linked, but genetically unlinked, and behave as if found on different chromosomes

Lecture 8E
Chi Square for
Linked Genes
Chapter 5
Dr. Neil Voss
Oct 30, 2023
Are Two Genes Linked or Unlinked?
Chi-Square Test
for Linked Genes
Linked or Unlinked?
If genes are unlinked and assorting independently, the phenotypic ratio of offspring from an F1 testcross is expected to be 1:1:1:1.
If the two genes are linked, however, the observed phenotypic ratio of the offspring will not match that ratio. 
Given that random fluctuations in the data do occur, how much can the number deviate from the expected numbers for us to conclude that they are different?
Chi-Square (2) Test
Compares an observed data set to an expected data set predicted by a hypothesis and measures the discrepancy between the two.
Thus, determining a goodness of fit
If the discrepancy between the observed and expected data sets is so large that it is unlikely to have occurred by random fluctuation, we say there is statistically significant evidence against the hypothesis.
or, more specifically, evidence for the genes being linked.
Chi-square analysis can be used to test for linkage
Cosmos Plant
In cosmos plants, purple stem (A) is dominant to green stem (a), and short petals (B) is dominant to long petals (b). 
In a simulated cross, AABB plants were crossed with aabb plants to generate F1 dihybrids (AaBb), 
which were then test-crossed (AaBb * aabb) total of 900 offspring plants were scored for stem color and flower petal length.

Figure 15.UN03b Skills exercise: using the chi-square test (part 2)
Chi-Square (2) Test
The 2 value means nothing on its own.
It is used to find the probability that the observed data set could have resulted from random fluctuations.
A low probability suggests that the observed data are not consistent with the hypothesis and the thus the hypothesis should be rejected.
A standard cutoff point used by biologists is a probability of 0.05 (5%). If the probability corresponding to the 2 value is 0.05 or less, the differences between observed and expected values are considered statistically significant and the hypothesis (that the genes are unlinked) should be rejected.
Likewise if the probability is above 0.05, the results are not statistically significant; the observed data are consistent with the hypothesis.
df = 3
2 = Sum
2.142
As the chi-squared statistic of 2.14 is less than critical value of 7.82, we accept the null hypothesis and conclude that the genes are unlinked.
What if you believe them to be linked despite the results?
What do you do?

Confusing Allele/Genotype Nomenclature
Here the crossing-over did not occur between the two genes. As a result, all four gametes are non-recombinant.
Missed Recombination #1:End crossover
Four non-recombinant gametes are produced
Missed Recombination #2:Double crossover
The result of two crossovers in the interval between two genes is indistinguishable from independent assortment of the genes, provided that the chromatids participate at random in the exchanges.
Results of multiple cross-overs depend on the number of strands involved
A two-strand double crossover that spans the middle pair of alleles in a triple heterozygote results in a reciprocal exchange of the middle pair of alleles between the two participating chromatids.
Three-point crosses to detect two-strand double cross-overs

Lecture 9B
Crossover Interference
Chapter 5
Dr. Neil Voss
Nov 6, 2023
Wait 9%+9.5% = 18.5%  17%
Chromosome
Recombinationfrequencies
9.5%
<number>
Figure 15.11 Research method: constructing a linkage map
Genetic Interference
Interference: A phenomenon where one crossover event on a chromosome decreases the likelihood of another crossover nearby.
Reduced Crossovers: When crossovers are close together, they can stop each other from happening.
Non-Random Recombination: This process doesn't happen just; it's a specific pattern that disrupts normal crossover rates.
Changes Expected Ratios: The usual expected mix of offspring traits according to simple genetics rules gets altered.
Measuring Interference: Scientists use special calculations like Coefficient of Coincidence (CoC) to find out how much interference is happening.
Effects on Gene Maps: Interference is important for accurate mapping of genes, as it affects how often genes are exchanged during reproduction
Step 5: Find distance between genes
distance between lz and gl genes
Expected: 10.7 + 14.7 = 25.4 m.u.
176 of 740 have crossovers between lz and gl.
40+33+59+44
176/740 = 23.7 m.u.
6 are double cross-overs that are usually not counted.
176 + 6x2 = 188
188/740 = 25.4 m.u.
how does this work...
Interference
probability of crossover between lz and su was 0.107 (10.7 map units)
probability of crossover between su and gl was 0.147 (14.7 map units)
expected double crossover rate:
0.107  0.147 = 0.0157
0.0157  740 offspring = 11.6 double crossovers
only 6 double crossovers were observed.
less double crossovers than expected is called interference
coefficient of coincidence = 6/11.6 = 51.7%
interference = 1  coc = 100  51.7 = 48.3%
Interference Calculation Lz/Su/Gl
Probability of Independent Crossovers: Multiply individual probabilities of crossovers between genes Lz/Su and Su/Gl.
Calculation: 0.107 (Lz/Su)  0.147 (Su/Gl) = 0.015729
Expected Double Crossovers: Apply the combined probability to a total progeny counts (e.g., 1000 progeny).
Expected Number: 0.015729  740 = 11.648.5
Coefficient of Coincidence (CoC): Ratio of observed to expected double crossovers.
Calculation: 6 (observed) / 11.64 (expected) = 0.5155 or 51.55%
Interference: Measure of the reduction in expected crossovers due to interference.
Calculation: Interference: 1 - CoC = 1  0.5155 = 0.485 or 48.5%
This indicates a 48.5% decrease in expected double crossovers, showing no interference.
A mapping function
A mapping function corrects for the loss of detectable recombinants due to multiple cross-overs
chromosome interference occurs when crossing-over reduces the probability of a second cross-over nearby
Roles of
Heterochromatin and Euchromatin
Here, heterochromatin accounts for 2.8% of the total map length but is ~25% of the physical length.
Less Recombination inheterochromatin than euchromatin
Heterochromatin and euchromatin
Heterochromatin refers to regions of the chromosome that have a dense, compact structure in interphase.
Euchromatin is visible only after chromosome condensation in mitosis or meiosis.
Interference inDifferent Chromatin Regions
Chromatin Structure Impact: The compactness of heterochromatin restricts genetic recombination, unlike the more accessible euchromatin.
Due to its tightly packed structure, heterochromatin has fewer crossovers, demonstrating higher interference.
Being loosely packed, euchromatin allows more frequent crossovers, indicating lower interference.
Functional Dynamics: Gene mapping uncovers the functional implications of chromatin structure on gene expression and regulation. It shows specific regions where genes are active or silent.
Gene maps reveal sparse gene activity within heterochromatinunseen in traditional stains.
Interference patterns help identify boundaries of heterochromatin regions where crossovers are suppressed.
Sample Problems
Chromosome
Recombinationfrequencies
From the table on the right the total offspring was 3000 and there were 54 double crossovers. The gene distances are reported above. 
What is the interference?
Chromosome
Recombinationfrequencies
0.12 * 0.15 * 3000 = 54
number of double crossovers = 54
there is no interference
Interference Calculation ACB
Probability of Independent Crossovers: Multiply individual probabilities of crossovers between genes A/C and C/B.
Calculation: 0.12 (A/C)  0.15 (C/B) = 0.018
Expected Double Crossovers: Apply the combined probability to a total progeny counts (e.g., 1000 progeny).
Expected Number: 0.018  3000 = 54
Coefficient of Coincidence (CoC): Ratio of observed to expected double crossovers.
Calculation: 54 (observed) / 54 (expected) = 1.0 or 100%
Interference: Measure of the reduction in expected crossovers due to interference.
Calculation: Interference: 1 - CoC = 1  1.0 = 0.0 or 0%
This indicates a 0% decrease in expected double crossovers, showing no interference.

Your Turn
What is the interference ratio for this problem?
probability:0.08  0.12 = 0.0096
expected:0.0096  1000 = 9.6
coefficient:6/9.6 = 62.5%
interference:1  0.625 = 37.5%
Interference Calculation FGH
Probability of Independent Crossovers: Multiply individual probabilities of crossovers between genes F/G and G/H.
Calculation: 0.08 (F/G)  0.12 (G/H) = 0.0096
Expected Double Crossovers: Apply the combined probability to a total progeny counts (e.g., 1000 progeny).
Expected Number: 0.0096  1000 = 9.6
Coefficient of Coincidence (CoC): Ratio of observed to expected double crossovers.
Calculation: 6 (observed) / 9.6 (expected) = 0.625 or 62.5%
Interference: Measure of the reduction in expected crossovers due to interference.
Calculation: Interference: 1 - CoC = 1 - 0.625 = 0.375 or 37.5%
This indicates a 37.5% decrease in expected double crossovers, illustrating the impact of interference.

Lecture 9C
Tetrad Analysis
Chapter 5
Dr. Neil Voss

Mitotic Cross-over
Mitotic Cross-Over
Recombination can also occur during mitosis.
Unlike what happens in meiosis, mitotic crossovers are initiated by mistakes in chromosome replication or DNA damage.
As a result, mitotic recombination is a rare event
occurring no more frequently than once in a million somatic cell divisions.
Nonetheless, the growth of a yeast colony or a multicellular organism involves so many cell divisions, they can be detected.
Mitotic recombination can lead to twin spots
Tetrads
Products of Meiosis
With Drosophila, mice, peas, people, and other diploid organisms, each individual represents only  potential gametes generated from meiosis. 
Thus, until now, linkage mapping has been from examining the phenotypes of diploid progeny. 
For such diploid organisms, we do not know the connection between individuals and parental gametes. 
In contrast, some fungi provide maintain all four haploid products of meiosis in a single sac called an ascus.
These haploid cells survive as viable individuals that grow and perpetuate themselves by mitosis.
The phenotype of such haploid fungi is a direct representation of their genotype, without complications of dominance.
Saccharomyces cerevisiae
(unordered tetrads)
Life cycle of S. cerevisiae
S. cerevisiae mating
The yeast Saccharomyces cerevisiae is a simple single celled eukaryote with both a diploid and haploid mode of existence.
The mating of yeast only occurs between haploids, which can be either the a or  (alpha) mating type
haploid yeast can switch mating type as often as every cell cycle.
Life cycle of the budding yeast (Saccharomyces cerevisiae).

How to get the

(derivation)
Recombination Frequency
Now, what do these types tell us about linkage?
You will notice from examining the three types that only the NPD and TT types contain recombinants
they are key classes in determining recombinant frequency.
The NPD class contains only recombinants, whereas half the spores in the TT class are recombinant.
Therefore, we can write a formula for determining recombination frequency (RF) by using tetrads, where TT and NPD represent the percentages of those classes:
Recombination Frequency
Traditionally scientists have written a formula for determining RF by using tetrads:
But this is WRONG, just as with other linkage analyses, this value is an underestimate
because it does not consider double recombinants and other higher-level crossovers.
Nevertheless, the frequencies of PD, NPD, and TT can be used to make a correction for doubles.
Using tetrad analysis to correct map distance for double crossovers
Crossover Nomenclature
NCO, non-crossover meioses;
SCO, single-crossover meioses;
DCO, double-crossover meioses
classified according to whether they contain two genotypes (ditypes) or four genotypes (tetratypes, represented by TT).
NCO, non-crossover meioses; SCO, single-crossover meioses; DCO, double-crossover meioses
classified according to whether they contain two genotypes (ditypes) or four genotypes (tetratypes, represented by TT).
NCO, non-crossover meioses; SCO, single-crossover meioses; DCO, double-crossover meioses
Four common situations:
No cross-overs or 2-strand double cross-overs result in parental ditype (PD) asci. 
One recombination between the genes results in tetratype (TT) asci.
Three-strand double cross-overs give the same result as a single crossover, tetratype (TT) asci. 
Four-strand double cross-overs give non-parental ditype (NPD) asci. 
As a result, for linked genes,
PD >> NPD.
Tetrad analysis for linked genes in unordered tetrads
Triples and higher numbers of crossovers might occur, but we may assume that such crossovers are rare and therefore negligible.
NCO, noncrossover meioses; SCO, single-crossover meioses; DCO, double-crossover meioses
The key to the analysis is the NPD class
arises only from a double crossover between all four chromatids.
Because we are assuming that double crossovers occur randomly between the chromatids, we can also assume that the frequencies of the four DCO classes are equal. 
This assumption means that the NPD class should contain  of the DCOs, and therefore we can estimate that
The single-crossover class also can be calculated by a similar kind of reasoning.
Notice that tetratype (TT) asci can result from either single-crossover or double-crossover meioses.
But we can estimate the component of the TT class that comes from DCO meioses to be 2NPD.
Hence, the size of the SCO class can be stated as
Now that we have estimated the sizes of the SCO and DCO classes, the non-crossover class can be estimated as
Thus, we have estimates of the sizes of the NCO, SCO, and DCO classes in this marked region. 
We can use these values to derive a value for m, the mean number of crossovers per meiosis in this region.
We can calculate the value of m simply by taking the sum of the SCO class plus twice the DCO class (because this class contains two crossovers). Hence:
To convert an m value into map units, m must be multiplied by 50 because each crossover on average produces 50 percent recombinants.
adjusted for double crossovers

recombination frequency?

parental ditype (PD) tetrad
AB AB ab ab
only parental genotypes are observed
nonparental ditype (NPD) tetrad
Ab Ab aB aB
only recombinant genotypes are observed
tetratype (TT) tetrad
AB Ab aB ab
all four possible genotypes are present
Tetrad Analysis
Triples and higher numbers of crossovers might occur, but we may assume that such crossovers are rare and therefore negligible.
NCO, noncrossover meioses; SCO, single-crossover meioses; DCO, double-crossover meioses
adjusted for double crossovers
Map Distance
The map distance for tetrads is given by the formula:
with parentheses and below without:
Tetrad Analysis
with Three (3) Genes
Steps to Solve aUnordered Tetrad Problem
The yeast Saccharomyces cerevisiae has unordered tetrads. A cross is made to study the linkage relationships among three genes.
Step 1: Find the Parental Ditypes (PD) for all three genes.
Step 2: Pick any two genes (for example, a,b)
Step 3: Determine if the two genes are linked.
PD  NPD  linked; PD  NPD  unlinked
Step 4: Determine the map distance between the two genes
D =  (TT + 6 NPD)/total = (3 NPD + TT)/total x 100%
Step 5: Go to Step 2 and pick a new pair of genes until all pairs are complete.
The yeast Saccharomyces cerevisiae has unordered tetrads.

Another Example,
you can do this yourself.
For each pair of genes, classify the tetrads as PD, NPD and TT, and tabulate the results as follows:
leu2  trp1 (l and t)
PD: 230 + 235 = 465
NPD: 215 + 220 = 435
TT: 54 + 46 = 100
For this pair of genes, PD = NPD, therefore, leu2 and trp1 are unlinked.
For each pair of genes, classify the tetrads as PD, NPD and TT, and tabulate the results as follows:
leu2  met14 (l and m)
PD: 235 + 220 = 455
NPD: 230 + 215 = 445
TT: 54 + 46 = 100
For this pair of genes, PD  NPD, therefore, leu2 and met14 are unlinked.
For each pair of genes, classify the tetrads as PD, NPD and TT, and tabulate the results as follows:
trp1  met14 (t and m)
PD: 235 + 215 + 46 = 496
NPD: 230 + 220 + 54 = 504
TT: = 0
For this pair of genes, PD = NPD and therefore, trp1 and met14 are also unlinked.
However, there are no TT for trp1 and met14, which means that both genes are closely linked to their respective centromeres.

Abnormalities
We study some of the chromosomal abnormalities encounter in human beings and other animals and plants
In general animals are much less tolerant of chromosomal changes than are plants.
Duplication of entire sets of chromosomes is not necessarily harmful in plants.
Sets of Chromosomesin Human Cells
Human somatic cells have 23 pairs of chromosomes
A karyotype is an ordered display of the pairs of chromosomes from a cell

Q. You are preparing a karyotype for a patient, during which phase of cell division do you take a snapshot of the cell?
A. anaphase
B. interphase
C. metaphase
D. prometaphase
E. telophase
Answer: C
A karyotype is a photograph of an individual's chromosomes arranged in pairs and arranged according to their lengths. 
The photograph is taken during mitosis when all of the chromosomes have been duplicated and condensed.
Prophase they are in a bunch and usually not defined enough.
The toxin colchicine is added to the cells preventing the spindles from binding to the centromeres, so cell division is stuck in metaphase. 
Two different ways to label the chromosomes:
1. Traditional staining
2. Chromosome painting
Traditional
Karyotype
Chromosome
Staining
1. Traditional staining  to treat chromosomes with a staining reagent called GIEMSA
which causes the chromosomes to exhibit bands, called G-bands which are specific to each pair of homologs.
Heterochromatic regions, which tend to be rich with adenine and thymine (AT-rich) DNA and relatively gene-poor, stain more darkly in G-banding.
Conversely, R-bands are less condensed chromatin, which tends to be rich with guanine and cytosine (GC-rich) and more transcriptionally active.
1. Traditional staining
Heterochromatin and euchromatin
Heterochromatin refers to regions of the chromosome that have a dense, compact structure in interphase.
Euchromatin is visible only after chromosome condensation in mitosis or meiosis.
G-bands are relatively gene poor 
R-bands are gene-rich
1. Traditional staining
In a human karyotype, chromosomes are arranged in 23 pairs. If we choose one of these pairs, such as pair 14, which of the following do the two chromosomes of the pair have in common?
More than one maybe correct.
A. length 
B. position of the centromere
C. staining pattern

Modern
Karyotype
Chromosome
Painting
2. Chromosome painting
2. Chromosome painting: Different colors are painted on each chromosome by hybridization with DNA strands labeled with different fluorescent dyes.
Fluorescence in situ hybridization (FISH) is a technique that uses fluorescent probes that bind to only those parts of the chromosome with a high degree of sequence complementarity.
The hybridization of multiple chromosome painting probes, each tagged with a specific fluorochrome results in the differential color display of chromosomes, i.e. color karyotyping.
Fluorescence in situ hybridization (FISH)
Multicolor FISH (mFISH) and Spectral karyotype (SKY technique)
How does SKY work?
Large collection of probes. Each probe is complementary to a unique region of one chromosome  together the "library" is complementary to all of the chromosomes within the human genome.
Each probe is labeled with a fluorescent color that is designated for a specific chromosome. 
For example, probes that are complementary to chromosome 1 are labeled with yellow molecules, while those that are complementary to chromosome 2 are labeled with red molecules, and so on.
Comparison
There are two slightly different imaging systems that utilize the polarized fluorescence filters to distinguish a mixture of chromosomal paints in a karyotype. 
multicolor FISH (mFISH; Speicher et al., 1996).
spectral karyotyping (SKY; Schrock et al., 1996)
Main difference:
one image acquisition with post-analysis in SKY versus sequential imaging with specific filters in mFISH.

Neanderthal Karyotype
The Story of
HeLa cells
a cell type in an immortal cell line used in scientific research. 
the oldest and most commonly used human cell lines.
derived from cervical cancer cells taken from HenriettaLacks
a patient who eventually died of her cancer on October 4, 1951.
remarkably durable and prolific
contaminated many other cell lines used in research
Cause of Ms. Lacks cancer
Human papillomavirus (HPV) causes 70% of all cervical cancers. But less than 10% cases become cancer.
Sexually transmitted disease. More than 80% of American women will have contracted HPV by age 50.
In 2006, a vaccine was approved for use. There are efforts to require teenage girls to get vaccinated.
Significance of HeLa cells
Discovered that HeLa cells did something they'd never seen before: They could be kept alive and grow.
First human cells that could be grown in a lab and were immortal. They did not die after a few cell divisions and could be used for conducting many experiments.
Used to develop polio vaccine and research cancer, understand AIDS, develop drugs the effects of radiation and toxic substances, and gene mapping.
Scientists have grown some 50 million metric tons of her cells.
this would fill all the skyscrapers in Chicago
HenriettaLacks
an poor AfricanAmerican woman without health insurance who was the unwitting donor of cells from her cancerous tumor in 1951.
In the early 1970s, the family started getting calls from researchers who wanted blood samples from them to learn the family's genetics. 
Family wondered why and this is when they learned about the removal of Henrietta's cells.
Family members are still confused about how Henrietta's cells could have been taken without her consent and how they could still be alive 50 years after her death.
HeLa Karyotype is crazy!!!

Tan = Genes
Gene density is not highly correlated with chromosome size!!!
Chromosomes 19 and 22 have the highest gene densities
Chromosomes 4 and 5 have the lowest gene densities
The short arm of a chromosome is called p and the long arm q. 
p = petite
q = not p

Dewey Decimal System for Genes
Geneticists use a standardized way of describing a gene's cytogenetic location. In most cases, the location describes the position of a particular band on a stained chromosome:
17q12
It can also be written as a range of bands, if less is known about the exact location:
17q12-q21
Gene Chromosomal Address
1. The first number or letter used to describe a gene's location represents the chromosome.
Chromosomes 1 through 22 (the autosomes) are designated by their chromosome number. 
The sex chromosomes are designated by X or Y.
7q31.2
Gene Chromosomal Address
2. The chromosome arm is the second part of the gene's address.
Choice of either q or p (petite)
For example, 5q is the long arm of chromosome 5, and Xp is the short arm of the X chromosome.
7q31.2
Gene Chromosomal Address
3. The position of a gene is based on the pattern of light and dark bands on the stained chromosome.
The position is usually designated by two digits (representing a region and a band)
sometimes it is followed by a decimal point and one or more additional digits.
The number indicating the gene position increases with distance from the centromere. 
For example: 14q21 represents position 21 on the long arm of chromosome 14. 14q21 is closer to the centromere than 14q22.
7q31.2
7  chromosome 7
q  long arm
31.2
region #3
band #1
sub-band #2

Lecture 10D
Chromosome Aneuploidy
Chapter 6
Dr. Neil Voss
Nov 13, 2023
Chromosome Alterations
Large-scale chromosomal alterations in humans and other mammals often lead to spontaneous abortions (miscarriages) or cause a variety of developmental disorders
Plants tolerate such genetic changes better than animals do
About 15% of all pregnancies terminate in spontaneous abortion.
many (~50%) due to chromosomal abnormalities
Euploid  organism where chromosome number is an exact multiple of the haploid number
balanced translocation  non-homologous chromosomes interchange parts, but all parts are present
Aneuploid  organism where chromosome number is NOT an exact multiple of haploid number
unbalanced translocation  some or part of the genome is missing.
Polyploid  organisms containing more than two paired (homologous) sets of chromosomes.
Chromosome Errors
Polyploid
Polyploid cells and organisms are those containing more than two paired sets of chromosomes. 
Polyploidy is found in some organisms and is especially common in plants.
Most Eukaryotes are diploid 
one set inherited from each parent.
Estimated that 3080% of living plant species are polyploid.
Human breeding and cultivation methods have tended to produce these variants.
Polyploid plants are usually unable to interbreed with their diploid ancestors
Polyploidy
Polyploidy is a condition in which an organism has more than two complete sets of chromosomes
Triploidy (3n) is three sets of chromosomes
Tetraploidy (4n) is four sets of chromosomes
Polyploidy is common in plants, but not in animals
Polyploids are more normal in appearance than aneuploids
Aneuploid
Aneuploid  organism where chromosome number is NOT an exact multiple of haploid number
Trisomy  diploid with extra copy of an individual chromosome
Monosomy  diploid with missing copy of an individual chromosome
trisomy, 21
Most Common Trisomy
Trisomy 13 (Patau Syndrome)
more than 80% dont survive more than a few weeks, very rarely live into their teens.
low birthweight, brain-structure problems, and undeveloped facial features
Trisomy 18 (Edwards Syndrome)
many babies with the condition don't survive to birth; of those who survive, less than 10% live to reach age 1.
born very small and frail, have many serious health problems and physical defects
Trisomy 21 (Down Syndrome)
mild to moderate issues with thinking, reasoning, and understanding; tend to have certain physical features in common
Trisomy 13 Trisomy 18
Down Syndrome
caused by the presence of an extra chromosome 21. In this case, instead of having two of chromosome 21, your baby will have three.
characterized by delayed physical and mental development.
Down syndrome is an aneuploid condition that results from three copies of chromosome 21
There are three types of Down syndrome: 
95% trisomy 21 (nondisjunction)
4% translocation accounts
1% mosaicism accounts
Down Syndrome Stats
<number>
Down syndrome is the most commonly occurring chromosomal condition.
Chromosomal trisomy has been reported to occur in at least 4% of all pregnancies
Trisomy 21 occurs in about 1 in 700 live births
Trisomy 21 is the only autosomal trisomy where the people survive to adulthood.
Down Syndrome Stats
<number>
Down syndrome occurs in people of all races and economic levels
Most people with Down syndrome lead healthy lives, but they have an increased risk for certain medical conditions 
such as congenital heart defects, respiratory and hearing problems, Alzheimers disease, childhood leukemia and thyroid conditions.
Down Syndrome Stats
<number>
Down Syndrome Incidence
Risk for having a baby with Downs Syndrome increases with maternal age.
Women who are 35 have a 1 in 350 chance of having a child with the syndrome
Women who are 45 have a 1 in 30 chance. 
Woman less than 30, have 1 in 1000. 
Affect of Mothers Age
The incidence of births of children with Down syndrome increases with the age of the mother.
The frequency of Down syndrome increases with the age of the mother, a correlation that has not been well explained
But due to higher fertility rates in younger women, 80% of children with Down syndrome are born to women under 35 years of age
Why is Age a Factor?
Based on mice studies...
There are proteins (cohesin and securin) present that help keep chromosomes together at their centers.
Lower levels of these proteins cause the sister strands to be more loosely connected and further apart.
Researchers found that older female mice had lower amounts of these proteins in their eggs.
Less of these proteins leads to instability in the chromosome pairs and a higher likelihood of nondisjunction.
^^ literally the only source I can find on the internet

Figure 15.15 Down syndrome
chromosomes underwent replication with no cell division, resulting in a tetraploid.
Prenatal Testing
Prenatal Triple Screen Test
The triple screen test is a maternal blood screening test that looks for three specific substances: AFP, hCG, and Estriol.
Low levels of AFP and abnormal levels of hCG and estriol may indicate that the developing baby has a chromosome abnormality. 
Nuchal translucency(NT) scan
prenatal ultrasound screening scan to detect chromosomal abnormalities
Performed between 11 and 14 weeks of gestation
Nuchal scanning over biochemical blood profiling has reduced false positive rates
Nuchal scanning alone detects 62% of all Down syndrome (sensitivity) with a false positive rate of 5.0%
Nuchal scanning combined with blood testing gives corresponding values of 73% and 4.7%.
When screening is positive, chorionic villus sampling (CVS) or amniocentesis testing is required to confirm the presence of a genetic abnormality
Invasive Fetal Testing
Amniocentesis, the liquid that bathes the fetus is removed and tested
Amniocentesis detects chromosome abnormalities, neural tube defects, and genetic disorders. Also provides access to DNA for paternity testing.
Chorionic villus sampling (CVS), a sample of the placenta is removed and tested
Also detects chromosome abnormalities (e.g. Down syndrome) and genetic disorders (e.g. cystic fibrosis). It does not allow for testing for neural tube defects.
Percutaneous umbilical blood sampling (PUBS), blood is removed from the umbilical cord

Percutaneous umbilical blood sampling (PUBS). This is also done in the second trimester using blood removed from the umbilical cord.
Newborn Screening
One common test is for phenylketonuria (PKU)
A recessively inherited disorder of phenylalanine metabolism
A PKU test is done after a baby is 24 hours old and after the baby has ingested protein to ensure accurate results.
Some genetic disorders can be detected shortly after birth by simple tests that are now routinely performed in most hospitals.
Blood test results usually are ready by the time a baby is 57 days old. Parents won't often hear about results if screening tests were normal.

Nondisjunction
Abnormal Chromosome Number
In nondisjunction, pairs of homologous chromosomes do not separate normally during meiosis
As a result, one gamete receives two of the same type of chromosome, and another gamete receives no copy

Aneuploidy
Aneuploidy results from the fertilization of gametes in which nondisjunction occurred
Offspring with this condition have an abnormal number of a particular chromosome
A monosomic zygote has only one copy of a particular chromosome (n  1)
A trisomic zygote has three copies of a particular chromosome (n + 1)
If cell X enters meiosis, and nondisjunction of one chromosome occurs in one of its daughter cells during meiosis II, what will be the result at the completion of meiosis?
A) All the gametes descended from cell X will be diploid.
B) Half of the gametes descended from cell X will be n + 1, and half will be n - 1.
C) 1/4 of the gametes descended from cell X will be n + 1, 1/4 will be n - 1, and 1/2 will be n. 
D) Two of the four gametes descended from cell X will be haploid, and two will be diploid.
Answer: C
A. Mother
B. Father
C. Cannot be determined
because the 45, X daughter contains color blindness, she must have received it from her father.
The nondisjunction must have occurred in the mother creating an egg cell lacking an X chromosome.
Question: Are adults with Down Syndrome (A) sterile?
or can they (B) reproduce?
Men with Down syndrome are commonly believed to be sterile.
As of 2005, there are scientific reports of four pregnancies that were fathered by three male Down syndrome patients.
Infertility in males has been attributed to defective spermatogenesis, but ignorance of the sexual act may be one of the contributing factors. (Pubmed: 17094988)
With Down syndrome individuals living longer into adulthood, we could see more men having offspring.
Women with Down syndrome can and do have children. 
What is the likelihood that their offspring will have Down syndrome?
Women with Down syndrome can and do have children. 
What is the likelihood that their offspring will have Down syndrome?
A. None, 0%
B. , 25%
C. , 50%
D. , 75%
E. All, 100%
Trisomic Segregation
If an individual with Down syndrome has a child, what is the likelihood that their child will also have Down syndrome?
Trisomic Segregation
a.k.a. how do three chromosomes line up at the metaphase plate?
When a trisomic individual mates with a normal individual, they are expected to produce a 1:1 ratio of normal to trisomic progeny.

Lecture 11B
Gene Trees
Chapter 19.1
Dr. Neil Voss
Nov 20, 2023
Why do we want to study molecular evolution?
Why Evolution Matters
How do new diseases emerge in a populations?
Why do disease persist in all living organisms?
What causes the diseases?
Why do pest under control resurge in high frequency?
Of course, evolutionary history
Why Evolution Matters
Understanding evolution is crucial for grasping the complexities of biological diversity.
Evolution explains the mechanisms behind biodiversity and species adaptation.
It underpins genetic and phenotypic changes across diverse species.
Studying evolution is key to comprehending the intricate history of life on Earth.
Highlight the significance of evolution in biology, emphasizing its role in explaining biodiversity and adaptation. Use Darwin's finches as an example to illustrate natural selection and adaptive evolution.
Gene Trees
Unraveling Gene Trees
Gene trees are visual representations that map genetic lineage and evolutionary relationships.
They differ from species trees by focusing on the evolutionary path of specific genes.
Essential tools in molecular genetics for tracing gene evolution across species.
Provide insights into how genetic variations contribute to evolutionary processes.
Interpreting Gene Trees
Gene trees help decode the relationships and common ancestry between different species.
The time axis in gene trees is crucial for understanding the timing of divergences.
These trees are instrumental in visualizing the evolutionary pathways of genes.
They provide a context for understanding genetic evolution within the broader scope of species evolution.

Reading a Gene Tree
Branches reflect evolutionary paths from common ancestors.
Each divergence event is marked by a branch point in the tree.
These points signify the splitting of one group into two distinct lines.
Interpretation of these points helps trace species' evolutionary history.
Understanding Branch Points
Closeness of species determined by recency of common ancestors.
To find the most recent common ancestor (MRCA), walk backwards to where species lines converge.
Example: A and B share a more recent ancestor than B and C, indicating closer relation.
Understanding MRCA is crucial for interpreting evolutionary relationships.
Tracing Common Ancestors
Different Forms, Same Gene Tree
The three trees above represent identical relationships among species A, B, C, D, and E.
The same evolutionary relationships can be represented in multiple tree forms.
Orientation of branches doesnt change the underlying genetic connections.
Rotating Branches in Trees
Another critical point is that if you rotate the structures, about the branch points as a pivot, you dont change the relationships.
So just like the two trees left, which show the same relationships, all of the trees below show the same relationships among four species.
Despite different appearances, the underlying relationships remain constant.
Trees, trees, and trees, oh my
Polytomy in Gene Trees
Polytomy: A branch point with three or more diverging species.
Indicates lack of sufficient information to determine precise branching order.
Common in complex evolutionary scenarios.

Gene Tree Methods
Distance-Based Methods
Distance-based methods rely on genetic distance calculations between sequences.
Simple and efficient, ideal for large datasets with many species.
UPGMA and Neighbor-Joining are common examples.
Effective for providing a general view of phylogenetic relationships.
Understanding Distance Matrices
Distance matrix: A table showing genetic distances between pairs of sequences.
Essential in distance-based phylogenetic methods like UPGMA and Neighbor-Joining.
Distances often calculated using methods like p-distance or Jukes-Cantor model.
Provides a simplified, quantifiable view of genetic relationships.
UPGMA Distance Method
UPGMA: Unweighted Pair Group Method with Arithmetic Mean.
Constructs tree by grouping sequences based on minimum distance.
Assumes a molecular clock: constant rate of evolution across all lineages.
Useful for simple, quick phylogenetic analysis with less complexity.
Neighbor-Joining Distance Method
Neighbor-Joining: A bottom-up clustering method for tree construction.
Does not assume a constant rate of evolution, unlike UPGMA.
Iteratively groups closest pair of sequences, recalculates distances.
Preferred for its accuracy in more complex phylogenetic analyses.
Requires more math equations than UPGMA.
Character-Based Methods
Analyze specific genetic characters or sequences.
Include methods like Maximum Parsimony and Maximum Likelihood.
Focus on evolutionary changes in genes or morphological features.
Provide detailed insights, suitable for various data complexities.
Character-based methods analyze specific genetic or morphological characters.
Distance-based methods calculate overall genetic distances between sequences.
Character-based (e.g., Maximum Parsimony, Maximum Likelihood) offer detailed insights.
Distance-based (e.g., UPGMA, Neighbor-Joining) are efficient for large datasets.
Choice depends on data complexity and desired level of detail in analysis.
The Parsimony Method
Seeks the simplest explanation with the fewest evolutionary changes.
Ideal for datasets where evolutionary events are relatively infrequent.
Minimizes assumptions about evolutionary rates and patterns.
Balances between simplicity and explanatory power in tree construction.
Parsimony Example
there are various trees that could explain the phylogeny of the sequences AAG, AAA, GGA, AGA including the two above.
parsimony prefers the first tree because it requires fewer substitution events
Maximum Likelihood in Phylogenetics
Evaluates probability of observed data under different tree hypotheses.
Utilizes statistical models to estimate tree likelihood.
Suitable for complex datasets requiring detailed evolutionary models.
Computationally intensive, offering nuanced understanding of relationships.
Bayesian Inference in Phylogenetics
Bayesian methods incorporate prior knowledge with observed data.
They estimate the posterior probability of trees using statistical models.
Highly flexible and can incorporate various types of evolutionary models.
Provides a probabilistic framework for tree estimation and hypothesis testing.

Distance Method
Gene Treesby Distance Method
Differences can be a measure of evolutionary separation.
Two problems with this assumption:
1. mutations are chance and differences are not really correlated with evolutionary time.
2. some mutations increase survival rate and have better chance of being fixed.

Gene trees may not coincide with species trees
A gene tree may not coincide with a species tree because of the sorting of polymorphic alleles in the different lineages.

data from a study comparing the DNA sequence of the alcohol dehydrogenase (Adh) gene in several fruit flies
There are 43 nucleotide sites with variable base pairs, as well as several sites where insertions or deletions have occurred. 
Although 18 variable sites occur within the 4 exons of the Adh gene, only 1 of these variations (at site 1,490) results in an amino acid change. 
<number>
Figure 23.4 Extensive genetic variation at the molecular level
Rapid Reproduction
Mutation rates are low in animals and plants
The average is about one mutation in every 100,000 genes per generation
Mutation rates are often lower in prokaryotes and higher in viruses
Mutations accumulate quickly in prokaryotes and viruses because they have short generation times
<number>
Rates of DNA Evolution
There are only 4 nucleotides, but there are 20 aasmaller number of subunits means changes at nucleotide sites are more likely than at amino acid sites
Again, nucleotide sites evolve at different rates depending on the function and position in the genome
Synonymous substitution  doesnt result in aa replacement (Third codon position)
Non-synonymous substitution  results in aa replacement (First and second position.)
Pseudogenes  fastest evolving then fourfold degenerate site
Fraction of sites that undergo a change in some designated interval of time
Ex: beta globin molecule between Mouse and Human is 1.23 aa replacement per aa site per billion years.
Mouse and Human lineages have been separated for 80 million years.
the last time shared a common ancestor
147 aa long Beta globin chain
About 29 different aa changes total or about 8 aa change in first 50 aa..
Rate of Protein Evolution
Molecular Clock
Homologous Genes
Orthologous genes
genes that are duplicated as an accompaniment to speciation and that retain the same function.
Paralogous genes
duplicated genes that take on new gene functions.
Origin of New Species
New genes come from preexisting genesraw material comes from duplications of the regions of the genome
Orthologous genes: Groups of genes or proteins from different organisms that have the same function are said to be orthologous
Paralogous genes: Two homologous sequences (e.g., sequences that share a common evolutionary ancestor) that diverged by gene duplication, as opposed to orthologs, which diverged by speciation
Specialization of paralogous genes by subfunctionalization
Gene duplication allows the paralogous copies to evolve more specialized functions.
Specialization of paralogs accompanying the loss of functional capabilities is called subfunctionalization.

Polyploidy in Plants

Dr. Neil Voss

Polyploidy is the presence of extra sets of chromosomes due to accidents during cell division
Polyploidy is much more common in plants than in animals
Polyploidy can produce new biological species in sympatry within a single generation
Polyploidy
<number>
Polyploidy
Polyploidy is a condition in which an organism has more than two complete sets of chromosomes
Triploidy (3n) is three sets of chromosomes
Tetraploidy (4n) is four sets of chromosomes
Polyploidy is common in plants, but not in animals
Polyploids are more normal in appearance than aneuploids
Polyploid
Polyploid cells and organisms are those containing more than two paired sets of chromosomes. 
Most Eukaryotes are diploid one set inherited from each parent.
Polyploidy is found in some organisms and is especially common in plants.
Estimates suggest that 3080% of living plant species are polyploid.
Human breeding and cultivation methods have tended to produce these variants.
Usually unable to interbreed with their diploid ancestors
Monoploid vs. Haploid
In a decaploid Chrysanthemum with 90 chromosomes, what are the monoploid and haploid numbers?
Answer:
monoploid: 90/10 = 9 chromosomes
haploid: 90/2 = 45 chromosomes
Important Crops are Polyploids
At least five new plant species have originated by polyploid speciation since 1850
For example, in the genus Tragopogon, two allopolyploid species have evolved from three diploid parent species
Many important crops are polyploids:
corn, apples, bananas, coffee, sugar cane, peanuts, oats, cotton, potatoes, tobacco, and wheat
<number>
Why do humans make polyploid plants? Humans have been doing so since ancient times.
Polyploids are bigger!!!

like watermelon or bananas?

Autoploidy and Alloploidy
An autopolyploid is an individual with more than two chromosome sets, derived from a single species
An allopolyploid is a species with multiple sets of chromosomes derived from different species
A combination of chromosome sets from different species.
Allopolyploid plant species are much more numerous than autopolyploids.

Monoploid systems
This can be done by treatment with colchicine, which inhibits formation of the mitotic spindle.
Production of a diploid from a monoploid by treatment with colchicine.
Polyploidy
in Animals
Polyploidy is Not Tolerated in Animals
Rare in Animals: Studies of fossils show that polyploidy is very rare in animals, meaning it hasn't been a successful way for animals to evolve. Unlike in plants where it's common and often helpful. 
More Complex in Animals: Animals have complex organ systems and developmental processes. Polyploidy can cause serious problems in these systems, leading to major health issues.
Larger Cell Size: Polyploid cells are bigger. Animal cells become too large with polyploidy. This can interfere with how cells and tissues work in animals, causing health problems.
Gene Control: Animals need a careful balance of genes. Extra genes in animals lead to problems in controlling how genes work. In plants, this is less of an issue.
Cancer Risk: In animals, polyploidy can increase cancer risk. Cells with too many genes can lead to the growth of tumors. This is not a common problem in plants.
Question: Can you purchase GMO (genetically modified) animal meat in stores in the USA?
A. Yes, GMO meat is commonly found and regularly eaten in the U.S.
B. Yes, GMO meat is available, but I choose not to buy it due to health concerns.
C. No, stores refuse to stock any GMO meat because consumers are not willing to buy it.
D. No, selling GMO meat is hard in the U.S. because of strict rules and labeling needs.
E. No, it is against the law to sell GMO meat in the U.S., as it's not safe to eat.

Generalization of Sex Determination
Dosage Compensation
For organisms with XY sex determination, there is a problem with dosage of genes on the X and Y.
Dosage compensation has evolved to correct the unequal dosage in the sexes.
Dosage Compensation describes the process organisms use to equalize the expression of genes between different biological sexes.
C. elegans = nematode worms
In females, each X chromosome expression is reduced by half.
Both X's have the same activity levels
In males, a protein complex on the Y, increases transcriptional activity of the X.
The single male X does twice the work to equal the XX of the female.
Drosophila = fruit fly
In mammalian females, one of the two X chromosomes in each cell is randomly inactivated during embryonic development 
The inactive X condenses into a Barr body
Normally, the inactivated X is random.
A single organism will have different inactive X's throughout their body.
Heterozygous genes on the X chromosome, will produce a mosaic for that character.
Marsupials always deactivate the paternal X.
X Inactivation in Mammals
<number>
X inactivation
X-inactivation
Some of the genes that escape inactivation have function homologs on the Y chromosome.
about 15% of X genes escape inactivation
Mosaicism
Mosaicism can be observed directly in women who are heterozygous for an X-linked recessive mutation that results in the absence of sweat glands
These women exhibit patches of skin in which sweat glands are present and other patches of skin in which sweat glands are absent.
Calico Cat
Males have only one X chromosome, a normal male has either the orange or the black allele. 
A female can be heterozygous for orange and black will be calico
a mosaic of orange and black patches mixed with patches of white.
X chromosomes

Allele forblack fur

X inactivation and the tortoiseshell cat. 
Two different alleles, one for orange fur and one for black fur. 
If a female cat is heterozygous for the tortoiseshell gene, she is tortoiseshell.
Orange patches are formed by populations of cells in which the X chromosome with the orange allele is active; black patches have cells in which the X chromosome with the black allele is active.
<number>
Figure 15.8 X inactivation and the tortoiseshell cat
Calico Cat
The white patches are due to an autosomal gene S for white spotting
which prevents pigment formation in the cell lineages in which it is expressed. 
Why the S gene is expressed in some cell lineages and not others is not known.
Homozygous SS cats have more white than heterozygous Ss cats.
Summary of Dosage Compensation
Sex-Determining Region
on the Y chromosome (SRY)
for Mammals
Sex Chromosomes
Humans use the XY chromosomal system to determine sex.
if a Y chromosome is present during development (XY), the fetus will become a male
if there is no Y present (XX), the fetus becomes female
Pseudoautosomal Region
In humans, the tip of the short arm of the Y is homologous with a region at the tip of the short arm of the X chromosome. 
The shared XY homology defines the pseudoautosomal region
8.1 Hold the chromosome together for proper orientation in metaphase I.
8.2 alleles on the X can be interchanged with homologous alleles on the Y. Similar to autosomal inheritance.
Sex-determining Region on the Y
Short segments at the ends of the Y chromosomes are homologous with the X, allowing the two to behave like homologues during meiosis in males
A gene on the Y chromosome called SRY (Sex-determining Region on the Y) is responsible for development of the testes in an embryo
<number>
The Y chromosome has about 26 protein genes that are important for male fertility.
The Y chromosome contains the master sex-controller gene SRY at position Yp11.3.
SRY codes for a protein transcription factor, the testis-determining factor (TDF), which triggers male embryonic development

Aneuploidy of Sex Chromosomes
What would be the sex of an XXY human? And why?
A) male
B) female
C) cannot be determined
Answer: A
Nondisjunction of sex chromosomes produces a variety of aneuploid conditions
XXX females are healthy, with no unusual physical features
Klinefelter syndrome is the result of an extra chromosome in a male, producing XXY individuals
Monosomy X, called Turner syndrome, produces X0 females, who are sterile; it is the only known viable monosomy in humans 
Aneuploidy of Sex Chromosomes
<number>
Female Sex Abnormalities  45,X0
Turner's syndrome  occurs when females inherit only one X chromosome  their genotype is X0
short in stature, adult average 4' 7
often have distinctive webbed necks (i.e., extra folds of skin), small jaws, and high arched palates.
lack prominent female secondary sexual characteristics.
exceptionally small, widely spaced breasts, broad shield-shaped chests, and turned-out elbows. 
ovaries do not develop normally and are sterile.
between 1 in 2,000 to 1 in 5,000 female births
Female Sex Abnormalities  47,XXX
Triple-X syndrome  occurs in women who inherit three X chromosomes  their genotype is XXX (or more rarely XXXX or XXXXX).
As adults, these "super-females" generally are an inch or so taller than average with unusually long legs and slender torsos, but otherwise appear normal.
They tend to be emotionally immature for their size during childhood. This sometimes results in teachers and other adults labeling them as troublemakers.
1 in 1,000 female births
Approximately 100 cases of XXXX have been reported worldwide
Approximately 25 females have been diagnosed with XXXXX syndrome.
Male Sex Abnormalities  47,XXY
Klinefelter syndrome  males inherit one or more extra X chromosomes  their genotype is XXY (or more rarely XXXY or XY/XXY mosaic).
high-pitched voices
asexual to feminine body contours
as well as breast enlargement, and comparatively little facial and body hair.
sterile or nearly so
testes and prostate gland are small. 
Slightly above average height and likely to be overweight.
between 1 in 500 and 1 in 1000 male births
Male Sex Abnormalities  47,XYY
XYY syndrome  males inherit an extra Y chromosome  their genotype is XYY.
As adults, these "super-males" are usually tall (above 6 feet) and generally appear and act normal.
However, they produce high levels of testosterone.
During adolescence, they often are slender, have severe facial acne, and are poorly coordinated.
They are usually fertile and lead ordinary lives as adults. 
Many, if not most, are unaware that they have a chromosomal abnormality.
Between 1 in 900 and 1 in 2,000 male births
Richard Speck
an American mass murderer who systematically tortured, raped, and murdered 8 nurses from South Chicago Community Hospital in 1966
a Swiss endocrinologist suggested, based on Jacobs' unsubstantiated theory and Speck's height and acne that Speck might have XYY syndrome
It was really taken up by the popular press
XYY and Violent Crimes
In 1965 and 1966, British cytogeneticist Patricia Jacobs' hypothesis, that men with XYY syndrome are more prone to aggressive and violent behavior than males with the normal XY karyotype
Speculation even ran that Richard Speck had natural propensity to violence to because he had an extra Y chromosome. 
a Swiss endocrinologist suggested, based on Jacobs' unsubstantiated theory and Speck's height that Speck might have XYY syndrome
analysis revealed that Speck had a normal XY karyotype
fake news
In the mid-1970s, a Danish study showed that XYY men were not more likely to commit violent crimes
bias problems with original data were because they looked only a criminals and not the general population
Male Sex Abnormalities  48, XXYY
XXYY syndrome  sex chromosome anomaly in which males have an extra X and Y chromosome.
The appearance of at least one Y chromosome with a properly functioning SRY gene makes a male. Therefore, humans with XXYY are normally males. 
Mostly similar to 47, XYY super males.
additional learning disabilities and medical problems
one in every 18,00040,000 male births
(a) 47, XXY  1 Barr body
(b) 45, X  0 Barr bodies
(c) 47, +21  depends on sex
(d) 47, XYY  0 Barr bodies
(e) 47, XXX  2 Barr bodies
No, it would be expected that only  of the progeny to be wildtype.
Because half of their gametes would have two sex chromosomes.

Lecture 12D
Chapter 6
Chromosome Alterations:
(1) Deletions
Dr. Neil Voss
Nov 27, 2023
Alterations of
Chromosome Structure
Breakage of a chromosome can lead to four types of changes in chromosome structure
Deletion removes a chromosomal segment
Duplication repeats a segment
Inversion reverses orientation of a segment within a chromosome
Translocation moves a segment from one chromosome to another
Alterations ofChromosome Structure
<number>
Deletion
Duplication
Inversion
Translocation
Alterations of chromosome number and structure are associated with some serious disorders
Some types of aneuploidy appear to upset the genetic balance less than others, resulting in individuals surviving to birth and beyond
These surviving individuals have a set of symptoms, or syndrome, characteristic of the type of aneuploidy
Human Disorders Due to Chromosomal Alterations
<number>
One possible result of chromosomal breakage is for a fragment to join a non-homologous chromosome. What is this alteration called?
A) deletion
B) inversion
C) translocation
D) duplication
Answer: C
Deletions
Deletion Detectionin Humans
Deletions can be detected in humans with 23andMe like SNP microarray chips
If regions of SNPs are missing from the analysis, a deletion is likely present.
Disorders Caused by Deletions
The syndrome cri du chat (cry of the cat), results from a specific deletion in chromosome 5
A child born with this syndrome is severely intellectually disabled and has a catlike cry; individuals usually die in infancy or early childhood
<number>
Deletions:
how do they happen?
Deletions
Deletions are generally harmful.
the larger the deletion the greater the harm.
Formed in two ways:
chromosome breakage in two places and fused together with piece left out.
ectopic recombination  recombination with repeated sequences on same chromosome.
Ectopic Recombination
Deletion Detection
in Drosophila
Deletion Mutant with wildtype Phenotype
In lab settings, deletions can be detected by missing wildtype alleles in heterozygous organisms or by a test-cross.
Once a deletion is found, its size can be estimated by determining which genes were in the deleted region.
Test-cross
Punnett
square
Deletions
In lab settings, deletions can be detected by missing wildtype alleles in heterozygous organisms or by a test-cross.
Once a deletion is found, its size can be estimated by determining which genes were in the deleted region.
Using Polytene Chromosomes to Find Deleted Regions
Drosophila chromosomes
Polytene Chromosomes
Polytene chromosomes are giant chromosomes 
They begin as normal chromosomes, but through repeated rounds of DNA replication without any cell division, they become large, banded chromosomes 
It is possible to study deletions by physically looking at chromosomes
Polytene Chromosomes

Deletion Quiz Question #1
four genes
Deletion Quiz Question
A total of four genes A, B, C, and D are closely linked in a single chromosome, but their order is unknown. 
A total of three deletions in the region are found to uncover recessive alleles of the genes as follows: 
Deletion #1 uncovers the 2 genes C and D; 
Deletion #2 uncovers the 3 genes A, C, and D; 
Deletion #3 uncovers the 3 genes A, B, and C; 
What the correct order for the four genes? 
Hint: the first gene on the end is gene B.

Deletion #3 uncovers the three genes A, D, and F; 
Deletion #4 uncovers the four genes A, C, D, and F; 
Deletion #5 uncovers the four genes A, B, C, and E;

Blackboard Assignment
This weeks lecture assignment includes two deletion questions
and a translocation meiosis question, if we get that far today.
Challenge Yourself
A total of nine genes A, B, C, D, E, F, G, H, and J are closely linked in a single chromosome, but their order is unknown.
A total of six deletions in the region are found to uncover recessive alleles of the genes as follows: 
Deletion #1 uncovers the five genes A, D, E, F, and H; 
Deletion #2 uncovers the four genes B, D, G, and J; 
Deletion #3 uncovers the five genes B, D, E, F, and H; 
Deletion #4 uncovers the four genes A, C, E, and H; 
Deletion #5 uncovers the two genes B and G; 
Deletion #6 uncovers the five genes B, D, E, F, and G; 
What the correct order for the nine genes? 
Hint: the first gene on the end is gene C.
Really Challenge Yourself
A total of twenty genes A, B, C, D, E, F, G, H, J, K, M, P, Q, R, S, T, W, X, Y, and Z are closely linked in a single chromosome, but their order is unknown. 
A total of seventeen deletions in the region are found to uncover recessive alleles of the genes as follows: 
Deletion #1 uncovers the five genes A, D, F, M, and Z; 
Deletion #2 uncovers the three genes B, J, and S; 
Deletion #3 uncovers the five genes E, F, H, M, and Y; 
Deletion #4 uncovers the five genes B, C, J, P, and S; 
Deletion #5 uncovers the two genes K and R; 
Deletion #6 uncovers the four genes C, J, P, and S; 
Deletion #7 uncovers the four genes A, D, R, and W; 
Deletion #8 uncovers the four genes E, F, M, and Z; 
Deletion #9 uncovers the five genes A, K, Q, R, and W; 
Deletion #10 uncovers the five genes C, G, P, S, and T; 
Deletion #11 uncovers the five genes E, F, M, Y, and Z; 
Deletion #12 uncovers the four genes C, G, P, and S; 
Deletion #13 uncovers the four genes C, G, P, and T; 
Deletion #14 uncovers the four genes B, J, P, and S; 
Deletion #15 uncovers the four genes E, H, M, and Y; 
Deletion #16 uncovers the four genes B, J, Q, and S; 
Deletion #17 uncovers the five genes C, G, P, T, and X; 
What the correct order for the twenty genes? 
Hint: the first gene on the end is gene H.
