Lecture 12E
Chromosome Abnormalities
(2) Duplications
Chapter 6
Dr. Neil Voss
Nov 27, 2023
Breakage of a chromosome can lead to four types of changes in chromosome structure
Deletion removes a chromosomal segment
Duplication repeats a segment
Inversion reverses orientation of a segment within a chromosome
Translocation moves a segment from one chromosome to another
Alterations ofChromosome Structure
<number>
Duplications
Duplication
Unequal Crossing Over
A tandem duplication is one in which the duplicated segment is in the same orientation immediately adjacent to the normal region in the chromosome.
Tandem duplications can produce more duplicated copies by unequal crossing over.
Duplications and Color blindness
Pigment genes in the X chromosome
Unequal Crossing Over
The red and green pigment genes are highly similar.
Unequal crossing over can occur
resulting in both deletion and duplication of the genes.
Green and Red Opsins
Comparison of the Amino Acid Sequences of the Green and Red Photoreceptors
Open circles correspond to identical residues, whereas colored circles mark residues that are different. 
The differences in the three black positions are responsible for most of the difference in their absorption spectra.
Rearrangements in the course of DNA replication may lead to (A) the loss of visual pigment genes or (B) the formation of hybrid pigment genes that encode photoreceptors with anomalous absorption spectra. 
Because the amino acids most important for determining absorption spectra are in the carboxyl-terminal half of each photoreceptor protein, the part of the gene that encodes this region most strongly affects the absorption characteristics of hybrid receptors.
Tetrachromats
Most birds and some insects are tetrachromats and can see some forms of Ultraviolet (UV)
Some human women are believed to have a second green cone receptor and therefore are tetrachromats
Human Tetrachromats
In humans, two cone cell pigment genes are located on the sex X chromosome.
Women have two X chromosomes with variant cone cell pigments
Possibly born as tetrachromats with four different kinds of cone cells
Tetrachromats
One study suggested that 23% of the world's women have a 4th cone between red and green cones, giving, an increase in color differentiation.
Another study suggests that as many as 50% of women and 8% of men may have four photopigments
Neuroscientist Gabriele Jordan has only been able to confirm the condition in one person in 20 years of research 
Unfortunately, computer screens do not provide enough color information to be able to 'tap into' the extra dimension that tetrachromats may possess. 
It is therefore impossible for an online test to investigate tetrachromacy. 
Sharp Quattron TVs
No longer sold in the US, only available in Asia
Evolutionary Relationships among Visual Pigments
Visual pigments have evolved by gene duplication along different branches of the animal evolutionary tree.
The branch lengths of the trees correspond to the percentage of amino acid divergence.
Mantis Shrimp, kings of vision
The vision of the mantis shrimp is so precise that it can perceive polarized light
Mantis Shrimp have 16 cones!!
Copy Number Variations (CNVs)
and disease
Copy NumberVariation (CNV)
CNV refers to the number of copies of a particular gene varies from one individual to the next.
CNVs can be generally categorized into two main groups: short repeats and long repeats
Gene Dosage
Although people generally think of mutations as a lack of function in a particular gene, CNVs that are duplications show that varying the gene dosage can also lead to disease. 
Genes are generally present in two copies, one on each chromosome, or 2n.
A deletion on a single chromosome leads to 1n, whereas duplication leads to 3n or more.
Huntingtons disease
short copy number variation is the tri-nucleotide repeat of the CAG base pairs in the huntingtin gene
once the CAG tri-nucleotide repeats more than 36 times, Huntingtons disease will likely develop in the individual
and it will likely be inherited by his or her offspring
Autism and
Schizophrenia
Gene Dosage
Duplications and deletions of the same region can often lead to different patterns of symptoms.
For example, deletion of 16p11.2 leads to autism and enlarged head size macrocephaly whereas duplication leads to symptoms that resemble schizophrenia and smaller head size (microcephaly). 
By contrast, duplication of another genetic region, 1q21.1, seems to favor autism and macrocephaly whereas deletion of the region causes schizophrenia and microcephaly.
Dup15q Alliance
Dup15q syndrome is a neurodevelopmental disorder characterized by having an extra copy of a portion of chromosome 15 in the 11.2  13.1 region in combination with a number of symptoms that may include hypotonia and motor delays, intellectual disability, autism spectrum disorder (ASD), and epilepsy, including infantile spasms.
22q Family Foundation
22q11.2 deletion syndrome is a chromosomal abnormality that can cause a wide range of health and developmental issues, including heart defects, breathing issues, problems with the gastrointestinal tract, immune and endocrine systems, differences in the palate, slow growth, autism/developmental delays or learning disabilities in some individuals.
1p36 Deletion Support & Awareness
1p36 Deletion Syndrome is a chromosome disorder that can be characterized by moderate to severe intellectual disability, delayed growth, limited speech ability and distinct facial features. It is estimated that 1p36 Deletion Syndrome occurs in one in every 5,000 to 10,000 births though many individuals still go undiagnosed. Though many people have never heard of 1p36 Deletion Syndrome, it is the one of the most common chromosome deletions.

Lecture 12F
Chromosome Abnormalities
(3) Inversions
Chapter 6
Dr. Neil Voss
Nov 27, 2023
Breakage of a chromosome can lead to four types of changes in chromosome structure
Deletion removes a chromosomal segment
Duplication repeats a segment
Inversion reverses orientation of a segment within a chromosome
Translocation moves a segment from one chromosome to another
Alterations ofChromosome Structure
<number>
Inversions
Inversion
Ectopic Recombination resulting in inversion
Blue and pink are inverted repeats
They couple in reverse orientation
Resulting in an inverted gene sequence
A B  Y Z 
 Z Y  B A
Homologous chromosome pairing with an inversion
In an organism that carries a normal chromo some and a inverted chromosome
the gene-for-gene attraction during synapsis causes
one of the chromosomes to form into a loop in the region in which the gene order is inverted.
No crossing over in an inversion loop = good
If there is no crossing over within the inversion loop, then the homologous chromosomes disjoin without problems.
Paracentric
Inversion
Paracentric Inversion
does NOT include the centromere
Paracentric (beside) Inversion
Anaphase I configuration resulting from the crossover.
Acentric and Dicentric chromosomes are formed
Pericentric
Inversion
Pericentric (around) Inversion
DOES include the centromere
Pericentric (around) Inversion
Anaphase I configuration resulting from the crossover.
Count the duplications and deletions
Summary
Paracentric Inversion  results in one dicentric chromosome and one acentric chromosome
Pericentric Inversion  results in two monocentric chromosomes with deletions and duplications of genes
single crossover:
[cen] A B c d a [cen] - dicentric
e b C D E - acentric
[cen] A B c D E
e b C d a [cen] or [cen] a d C b e
both monocentric!
How can we get the genotype: A B c D E?
Double crossover in the B-C and C-D regions
produces:
a b C d e and A D c B E
genotyping would hide the inverted gene order.
How can we get the genotype: A B c D E?
Hint: start with ancestral sequence (c); compare chromosomes pairwise to find those that differ by a single inversion.
comparing (c) to (d) we see an inversion in the I-d-c to c-d-i.
Now compare (d) to (a) and (b) to find a match with only one inversion
comparing (c) to (d) we see an inversion in the I-d-c to c-d-i.
comparing (d) to (a) we see an inversion of the h-g-c-d to d-c-g-h
comparing (c) to (d) inversion in the I-d-c to c-d-i.
comparing (d) to (a) inversion of the h-g-c-d to d-c-g-h
comparing (a) to (b) inversion of f-e-d-c to c-d-e-f
from this the evolutionary history is c  d  a  b
Position-effect
Variegation (PEV)
Position-effectVariegation (PEV)
Expression of a gene can be affected by its position on the chromosome.
most often whether a gene is in or near heterochromatin.
The Sparrow with Four Sexes
White Stripe
Tan Stripe
Remarkably, white birds of either sex almost exclusively mate with tan birds of the opposite sex.
A pattern which results in four effective sexes, as any given individual can breed with only one-quarter (25%) of the population
White and tan individuals also show alternative reproductive strategies:
White males are aggressive, provide little parental care, and are promiscuous, whereas tan males are monogamous and invest highly in rearing their nestlings
White females are more aggressive and solicit more copulations from their mates than do tan females
How Four Sexes
References
Supergenes: The Genomic Architecture of a Bird with Four Sexes (Feb 2016)
Divergence and Functional Degradation of a Sex Chromosome-like Supergene (Feb 2016)
The sparrow with four sexes (Nov 2016)
White Throated Sparrow Project

Lecture 12G
Chromosome Abnormalities
(4) Translocations
Chapter 6
Dr. Neil Voss
Nov 27, 2023
Breakage of a chromosome can lead to four types of changes in chromosome structure
Deletion removes a chromosomal segment
Duplication repeats a segment
Inversion reverses orientation of a segment within a chromosome
Translocation moves a segment from one chromosome to another
Alterations ofChromosome Structure
<number>
Translocations
Translocation
Translocations
(A) Two pairs of non-homologous chromosomes in a diploid organism.
(B) Heterozygous reciprocal translocation, in which two non-homologous chromosomes (the two at the top) have interchanged terminal segments. 
(C) Homozygous reciprocal translocation.
Semi-sterility
half the gametes will be incapable of contributing to the next generation, a condition known as semisterility or half sterility.
In plants, the 50 percent unbalanced meiotic products from segregation generally abort at the gametic stage.
In animals, however, the duplicationdeletion products are viable as gametes but lethal to the zygote. 
Certain cancers, including chronic myelogenous leukemia (CML), are caused by translocations of chromosomes
Disorders Caused by Translocations
<number>
Normal chromosome 9
Normal chromosome 22
Reciprocal translocation
Translocated chromosome 9
Translocated chromosome 22(Philadelphia chromosome)
The cancerous cells in nearly all Chronic myelogenous leukemia (CML) patients contain:
an abnormally short chromosome 22, the so-called Philadelphia chromosome, and 
an abnormally long chromosome 9. 
These altered chromosomes result from the reciprocal translocation shown here, 
which presumably occurred in a single white blood cell precursor undergoing mitosis and was then passed along to all descendant cells.
<number>
Figure 15.16 Translocation associated with chronic myelogenous leukemia (CML)
Really five questions:
(a) C-E inversion: A B E D C F G
(b) C-E deletion: A B F G
Reciprocal translocation of two chromosomes. 
My technique is add telomeres (**) to the ends of the chromosomes, because telomeres (**) cannot link up to the center of another chromosome.
**ABCDE|FG** and 
**MNOPQRS|TUV**
Reciprocal translocation of two chromosomes.
There are two possible ways for the translocation to happen
**ABCDE   FG** 
**MNOPQRS   TUV**
Reciprocal translocation of two chromosomes.
There are two possible ways for the translocation to happen
**ABCDE   FG** 
**MNOPQRS   TUV**
Reciprocal translocation of two chromosomes.
There are two possible ways for the translocation to happen
**ABCDE   FG** 
**MNOPQRS   TUV**
**ABCDE-SRQPONM** 
**GF-TUV**
Reciprocal translocation of two chromosomes.
There are two possible ways for the translocation to happen
**ABCDE   FG** 
**MNOPQRS   TUV**
Reciprocal translocation of two chromosomes.
There are two possible ways for the translocation to happen
**ABCDE   FG** 
**MNOPQRS   TUV**
**ABCDE-TUV** 
**MNOPQRS-FG**
For the reciprocal translocation of two chromosomes.
One product solution is...
**ABCDE-TUV** and **MNOPQRS-FG**
Another product solution is...
**ABCDE-SRQPONM** and **GF-TUV**
The telomeres are always attached to A, G, M, and V.
Reciprocal translocation of two chromosomes.
**ABCDE   FG** 
**MNOPQRS   TUV**
TUV**  FG**
Cannot happen because the telomeres would be in the center of the chromosome.
Last question: which product pairs are genetically stable?
Pair 1: **ABCDE-TUV** and **MNOPQRS-FG**
Pair 2: **ABCDE-SRQPONM** and **GF-TUV**
It depends on the location of the centromeres!
The problem does not tell us the centromere locations so lets pretend the centromeres are between C#D and P#Q
Pair 1: **ABC#DE-TUV** and **MNOP#QRS-FG**
Pair 2: **ABC#DE-SRQ#PONM** and **GF-TUV**
The problem does not tell us the centromere locations so lets pretend the centromeres are between C#D and P#Q
Pair 1: **ABC#DE-TUV** and **MNOP#QRS-FG**
Pair 2: **ABC#DE-SRQ#PONM** and **GF-TUV**
Pair 1 has both monocentric chromosomes, this is good.
Pair 2 has one dicentric and one acentric, this is genetically unstable
Another one so lets pretend the centromeres are between C#D and T#U
Pair 1: **ABC#DE-T#UV** and **MNOPQRS-FG**
Pair 2: **ABC#DE-SRQPONM** and **GF-T#UV**
Pair 1 has one dicentric and one acentric, this is genetically unstable 
Pair 2 has both monocentric chromosomes, this is good.
It switched!
Summary
(a) C-E inversion: A B E D C F G
(b) C-E deletion: A B F G
(c) A B C D E / T U V and M N O P Q R S / F G
or A B C D E / S R Q P O M N and V U T / F G
(d) two monocentric -OR- dicentric and acentric pair
(e) only monocentric chromosomes are stable
Typical Quiz Question
Q1. Two chromosomes with the gene sequences ABCDEFGHJ and MPQRSTWXYZ undergo a reciprocal translocation after breaks between B|C and T|W. 
Which one of the following is NOT a possible product of this translocation?
A. MPQRSTBA
B. MPQRSTCDEFGHJ
C. ZYXWCDEFGHJ
D. BAZYXW
E. ABWXYZ
Typical Quiz Question
Q1. Two chromosomes with the gene sequences
*ABCDEFGHJ* and *MPQRSTWXYZ* undergo a reciprocal translocation after breaks between B|C and T|W. 
Which one of the following is NOT a possible product of this translocation?
A. *MPQRSTBA*
B. *MPQRSTCDEFGHJ*
C. *ZYXWCDEFGHJ*
D. BA**ZYXW
E. *ABWXYZ*
Answer: D
Typical Quiz Question
Q2. Two chromosomes with the gene sequences ABCDEFGHJK and QRSTWXY undergo a reciprocal translocation after breaks between G|H and W|X. 
Which one of the following is NOT a possible product of this translocation?
A. YXGFEDCBA
B. ABCDEFGWTSRQ
C. YXHJK
D. KJHWTSRQ
E. HJKYX
Typical Quiz Question
Q2. Two chromosomes with the gene sequences *ABCDEFGHJK* and *QRSTWXY* undergo a reciprocal translocation after breaks between G|H and W|X. 
Which one of the following is NOT a possible product of this translocation?
A. *YXGFEDCBA*
B. *ABCDEFGWTSRQ*
C. *YXHJK*
D. *KJHWTSRQ*
E. HJK**YX
Answer: E

C. 1/2 will be wildtype and 1/2 will have the translocation.
D. All will carry the same translocation as the father.

A phenotypically normal prospective couple seeks genetic counseling because the man knows that he has a translocation of a portion of his chromosome 4 that has been exchanged with a portion of his chromosome 12.
Although his translocation is balanced, he and his wife want to know the probability that his sperm will be abnormal.
Assume that all gametes produce viable sperm, even if genetic material is missing
What is your prognosis regarding his sperm?
A. None will carry the translocation.
B. 1/4 will be normal, 
1/4 will have translocated chromosomes, and 
1/2 will have both normal and translocated chromosomes
C. 1/2 will be normal and 1/2 will have the translocation.
D. All will carry the same translocation as the father.
Answer: B
Robertsonian Translocations
Two non-homologous acrocentric chromosomes undergo fusion of their short arms
Important in human genetics
risk factor in Down syndrome when chromosome 21 is involved.
Genetically unstable
Chimpanzee
Gorilla  have
48 chromosomes
The proposed mechanism for the creation of the long human chromosome 2 by fusion of two shorter chromosomes
Which human chromosomes have the potential for a Robertsonian translocation?
13, 14, 15, 21 and 22 are the most acrocentic human chromosomes with potential for Robertsonian translocations
A Robertsonian translocation can join chromosome 21 to any other acrocentric chromosome
Adjacent gametes have missing portions and duplicate portions of the chromosomes
Alternate gametes have exactly 100% of the genome; but the chromosomes might be scrambled

Lecture 13C
Allele Frequencies
Chapter 17
Dr. Neil Voss
Dec 4, 2023
Definitions
Gene pool  Includes all alleles present in the population - total aggregate of genes
Phenotype frequency  proportion of individuals in a population that are a particular phenotype
example: population of flowers ..Red and white
Genotype frequency  proportion of individuals in a population that are a particular genotype.
example: Among flowers  RR, Rr and rr
A population is a localized group of individuals capable of interbreeding and producing fertile offspring
A gene pool consists of all the alleles for all loci in a population
A locus is fixed if all individuals in a population are homozygous for the same allele
Gene Pools and Allele Frequencies
<number>
Definitions
Allele frequency  proportion of all the alleles that are of specified type
example: allele frequency of R & allele frequency of r
Evolution  rate of change in frequency of alleles in a population
Natural Selection  differential success in the reproduction of different phenotypes resulting from the interaction of organisms with their environment.
Evolution occurs when natural selection (or other factors) causes changes in relative frequencies of alleles in the gene pool.
The frequency of an allele in a population can be calculated
For diploid organisms, the total number of alleles at a locus is the total number of individuals times 2 
The total number of dominant alleles at a locus is two alleles for each homozygous dominant individual plus one allele for each heterozygous individual; the same logic applies for recessive alleles
Allele Frequency
<number>
Population genetics studies groups of individuals and includes studies of
Allelic variation among individuals
Transmission of allelic variants from parents to offspring generation after generation
Temporal changes in the genetic makeup of a population due to systematic and random evolutionary forces
Population Genetics
<number>
CCR5 gene
Chemokine receptor gene (CCR5) found in human population
CCR5 receptor enables HIV virus infect CD4 class of T cells in immune system
it is necessary for HIV infection to progress.
10% humans contain CCR5 one allele known as 32
32 bps deletion within coding region
nonfunctional receptor
AIDS-resistance phenotype
Individuals homozygous (32/32) do not have functional CCR5 receptors and are resistant to HIV-1 infection
Heterozygotes have 2-3-year-slower progression to AIDS relative to wild types 
Partial CCR5 receptor gene sequence showing the 32 mutation
Chinese scientists are creating CRISPR babies
planned to eliminate a gene called CCR5 in hopes of rendering the offspring resistant to HIV, smallpox, and cholera.
CRISPR is employed to modify human embryos before they are transferred into womens uteruses
According to lead scientist He Jiankui, one couple in the trial gave birth to twin girls in November 2018
December 2019, the Shenzhen Nanshan District People's Court sentenced He to three years' imprisonment and a three-million-yuan fine.[18] He Jiankui was released from prison in April 2022
The birth of the first genetically tailored humans would be a stunning medical achievement, for both He and China. 
But it will prove controversial, too.
Where some see a new form of medicine that eliminates genetic disease, 
others see a slippery slope to enhancements, designer babies, and a new form of eugenics.

MN Blood Groups
ABO vs. MN blood group
All refer to different antigens on the surface of red blood cells.
System:
The ABO system refers to one set of antigens on red blood cells coded by the ABO gene. 
The corresponding antibodies are anti-A and anti-B antibodies. 
The MN blood group also refers to a different antigen controlled by co-dominant alleles.
Discovery:
The ABO blood groups system was discovered in 1900.
The MN groups system was discovered in 1927.
There are 346 red blood cell antigens and 33 platelet antigens
The M-N blood type is determined by two alleles of a gene on chromosome 4.
LM produces the M blood type.
LN produces the N blood type.
LM/LN heterozygotes have the MN blood type.
After the MN blood groups have been determined for a sample, allele frequencies can be calculated.
Estimating Allele Frequencies:
The MN Blood Type
<number>
Estimation of Allele Frequencies
What are the allele frequencies?
The total number of alleles is two times the sample size: 2  6129 = 12,258.
<number>
The frequency of the LM allele is 2 times the number of LMLM homozygotes plus the number of LMLN heterozygotes, all divided by the total number of alleles:
f(LM) = [(21787) + 3039] / 12,258 = 0.5395 = 54%
The frequency of the LN allele is 2 times the number of LNLN homozygotes plus the number of LMLN heterozygotes, all divided by the total number of alleles:
f(LN) = [(21303) + 3039] / 12,258 = 0.4605 = 46%
<number>
P,Q convention
By convention, if there are two alleles at a locus, p and q are used to represent their frequencies
The frequency of all alleles in a population will add up to 1
For example, p + q = 1
<number>
Letting p represent the frequency of the LM allele and letting q represent the frequency of the LN allele, we estimate that
p = 0.5395 and
q = 0.4605
Because LM and LN represent 100% of the alleles of this gene, p + q = 1.

f(A2) = 39/70 = 0.56

because dominant homozygotes would be identical to heterozygotes

When the second most frequent allele of a gene has a frequency greater than 0.01, the gene is polymorphic.

Lecture 13D
Hardy-Weinberg
Chapter 17
Dr. Neil Voss
Dec 4, 2023
Hardy-Weinberg principle
States that the frequencies of alleles and genotypes in a populations gene pool will remain constant over generations unless acted upon by agents other than Mendelian segregation and recombination of alleles.
allele
frequencies
dominant or
recessive
genotype
frequencies
homozygous or
heterozygous
allele
frequencies
dominant or
recessive
genotype
frequencies
homozygous or
heterozygous
The Hardy-Weinberg principle describes a mathematical relationship between allele frequencies and genotype frequencies and allows the prediction of a populations genotype frequencies from its allele frequencies.
Hardy-Weinberg Principle
<number>
Hardy-Weinberg Equation
The Hardy-Weinberg equation describes the genetic makeup we expect for a population that is not evolving at a particular locus
If the observed genetic makeup of the population differs from expectations under Hardy-Weinberg, it suggests that the population may be evolving
<number>
Hardy-Weinberg Equilibrium
In a population where gametes contribute to the next generation randomly and Mendelian inheritance occurs
allele and genotype frequencies remain constant from generation to generation
Such a population is in Hardy-Weinberg equilibrium
<number>
Two Mathematical Rulesof Hardy-Weinberg
p + q = 1
p = dominant allele frequency
q = recessive allele frequency
p2 + 2pq + q2 = 1
p2 = homozygote dominant
2pq = heterozygotes
q2 = homozygote recessive
<number>
If p and q represent the relative frequencies of the only two possible alleles in a population at a particular locus, then 
p2  2pq  q2  1
where p2 and q2 represent the frequencies of the homozygous genotypes and 2pq represents the frequency of the heterozygous genotype
allele
frequencies
dominant or
recessive
genotype
frequencies
homozygous or
heterozygous
<number>
Three Types of H-W Problems
Type 1:
Given all allele frequencies, p and q
Calculate expected genotype frequencies.
Type 2:
Given one or more genotype frequencies, p2
Calculate allele frequencies.
Type 3:
Given all genotype frequencies,
Determine whether or not population is evolving
uses Chi-Square
Wildflower Example
For example, imagine a population of 500 wildflower plants with two alleles, CR and CW, for a locus that codes for flower pigment. 
These alleles show incomplete dominance; thus, each genotype has a distinct phenotype. 
Incomplete dominance:
Plants homozygous for the CR allele (CRCR) produce red pigment and have red flowers; 
plants homozygous for the CW allele (CWCW) produce no red pigment and have white flowers; 
and heterozygotes (CRCW) produce some red pigment and have pink flowers.
Wildflower Example
<number>
Figure 23.UN01 In-text figure, incomplete dominance, p 484
Each allele has a frequency (proportion) in the population. 
For example, suppose our population has:
320 plants with red flowers
160 with pink flowers
20 with white flowers
Because these are diploid organisms, these 500 individuals have a total of 1,000 copies of the gene for flower color.

For example, consider a population of wildflowers that is incompletely dominant for color
320 red flowers (CRCR)
160 pink flowers (CRCW)
20 white flowers (CWCW) 
Calculate the number of copies of each allele
CR = (320  2) + 160 = 800
CW = (20  2) + 160 = 200
Allele Counts
<number>
Allele Frequency
To calculate the frequency of each allele
p = freq CR = 800 / (800 + 200) = 0.8
q = freq CW = 200 / (800 + 200) = 0.2
The sum of alleles is always 1
0.8 + 0.2 = 1
<number>
Apply Hardy-Weinberg
The probability of producing an AA homozygote is p  p = p2.
The probability of producing an aa homozygote is q  q = q2.
A heterozygote may be produced by
an A sperm uniting with an a egg or
an a sperm uniting with an A egg
Each of these events occurs with probability pq, so the total probability of forming an Aa zygote is 2pq.

CRCR: p2 = (0.8)2 = 0.64
CRCW: 2pq = 2(0.8)(0.2) = 0.32
(note: there are two of these)
CWCW: q2 = (0.2)2 = 0.04
The frequency of genotypes can be confirmed using a Punnett square
For the wildflowers

Tay-Sachs Disease
95% of all the alleles in the population are wildtype, but 5% of the alleles are the recessive allele which causes Tay-Sachs disease.
Assuming H-W, what frequency of the population would you expect to suffer from Tay-Sachs disease?
Using Allele Frequencies in Genetic Counseling
<number>
Cystic fibrosis is a genetic disorder in homozygous recessives that causes death during the teenage years. 
If 9 in 10,000 newborn babies have the disease, what are the expected frequencies of the dominant (A1) and recessive (A2) alleles according to the Hardy-Weinberg model?
A) f(A1) = 0.9997, f(A2) = 0.0003
B) f(A1) = 0.9800, f(A2) = 0.0200 
C) f(A1) = 0.9700, f(A2) = 0.0300 
D) f(A1) = 0.9604, f(A2) = 0.0392
Answer: C
Solution
q2 = 9/10000 = 0.0009
q = sqrt(0.0009) = 0.03
even number, what a surprise
p = 1-q = 0.97
f(A1) = 0.97; f(A2) = 0.03 =
Answer: C

PKU Hardy Weinberg
We know that frequency of homozygous recessive individuals (q2 in the Hardy-Weinberg theorem) = 1 in 10,000 or 0.0001.
q2 = 0.0001
The frequency of the recessive allele (q) is the square root of 0.0001 = 0.01.
q = 0.01
The frequency of normal alleles is
p = 1 - q = 1 - 0.01 = 0.99
The frequency of carriers is
2pq = 20.990.01 = 0.0198
or approximately 2% (1 in 50) of the U.S. population
<number>
Frequency ofHeterozygous Genotype
Frequencies:
1 in 10,000 are affected by PKU disorder
1 in 50 carry the PKU allele.
Important implication of Hardy-Weinberg principle is that
for a rare allele, the frequency of heterozygotes far exceeds the frequency of the rare homozygote.
Why is this the case? What does this mean?
Suppose 64% of a remote mountain village can taste phenylthiocarbamide (PTC) and must, therefore, have at least one copy of the dominant PTC taster allele. 
If this population conforms to Hardy-Weinberg expectations for this gene, what percentage of the population must be heterozygous for this trait?
A) 16%
B) 32%
C) 40%
D) 48%
Answer: D
Solution
1-0.64 = q2 
q = sqrt(0.36) = 0.6
p = 1-q = 0.4
2pq = 0.4*0.6*2 = 0.48

H-W for Three Alleles
For genes with multiple alleles, the Hardy-Weinberg genotype proportions are obtained by expanding a binomial expression.
Genes with Multiple Alleles

p1 + p2 + p3 = 1

Example: A-B-O Blood Type
The A-B-O blood types are determined by three alleles, IA, IB, and I, with frequencies p, q, and r, respectively.
Genotypes can be calculated by expanding the trinomial
(p + q + r)2 = p2 + q2 + r2 + 2pq + 2qr + 2pr

X-linked Alleles
H-W for X-Linked Genes
For X-linked genes, allele frequencies are estimated from the frequencies of the genotypes in males.
The frequencies of the genotypes in females are obtained by applying the Hardy-Weinberg principle to these estimated allele frequencies. This assumes that the allele frequencies are equal in males and females.
<number>
Males - Genotype frequency
is same as allele frequency
q2 affected
Allele and genotype frequencies for a X-linked gene in males and females
q affected
X-linked color blindness
So for an X-linked trait, the frequency of affected males provides an estimate of the frequency of the recessive allele.
It affects 1 in 20 males among Caucasians
So q= 1/20 = 0.05 
So frequency of color blind females
q2 = (0.05)2 = 0.0025 = 0.25% = 1 in 400

Lecture 13E
Genetics and Evolution
Chapter 17
Dr. Neil Voss
Dec 5, 2022
From this point forward, no more equations will appear on the exam.
Only definitions.
Genetics and Evolution
Evolution consists of progressive changes in the gene pool
progressive adaptation of a population to its environment
changes in the allele frequency
Four processes account for most changes in allele frequency:
Mutation
Migration/Gene flow
Natural Selection
Random Genetic Drift
Only natural selection causes adaptive evolution
The smaller a sample, the greater the chance of random deviation from a predicted result
Genetic drift describes how allele frequencies fluctuate unpredictably from one generation to the next
Genetic drift tends to reduce genetic variation through losses of alleles
Genetic Drift
<number>
Evolution by natural selection involves both chance and sorting
New genetic variations arise by chance
Beneficial alleles are sorted and favored by natural selection
Only natural selection consistently increases the frequencies of alleles that provide reproductive advantage
Natural Selection
<number>
Gene Flow/Migration
Gene flow consists of the movement of alleles among populations
Alleles can be transferred through the movement of fertile individuals or gametes (for example, pollen)
Gene flow tends to reduce genetic variation among populations over time
<number>
The introduction of genes by recent migrations can alter allele and genotype frequencies within a population and disrupt Hardy-Weinberg equilibrium.
Migration
<number>
Merged Populations
The merger of two randomly mating populations does not produce a population with Hardy-Weinberg genotype frequencies.
However, if the merged population mates randomly for one generation, Hardy-Weinberg genotype frequencies will be established.
<number>
Migration rates essentially work like Mutation rates, so we can treat them as one.
So, lets learn about mutations...
Mutation
Ultimate source of genetic variation  however the rate of mutation is low and harmful mutations are eliminated by natural selection. 
Low mutation rates are thought to be evolved as a compromise.
What do we mean by this?
Irreversible Mutation
Consider two alleles: A and a, suppose A undergoes mutation to a (Aa) at a rate  per generation
Proportion of A allele that do not mutate is 1-
If we let p and q represent frequencies of A and a
The current n-th generation has the frequency, pn
Allele frequency of A in the next (n+1)-th generation represents:
pn = pn-1 (1-)
Frequency of allele in the previous generation times proportion of alleles that failed to mutate in that generation.
Irreversible Mutation
A undergoes mutation to a (Aa) at a rate  per generation
p0 is the frequency of A in the original generation
Allele frequency of A in the n-th generation represents:
pn = p0 (1-)n 
Eventually, the generations increase p goes to zero.
p = p0 (1-)  0
At equilibrium, the population is then 100% allele a.
this is probably not realistic...
Reversible Mutations
and Equilibrium
Aa and aA
Reversible Mutation
Forward mutation of A  a is offset by reverse mutation of a  A.
Rate of forward mutation (Aa) is 
Rate of reverse mutation (aA) is  (greek nu)
At equilibrium (allele frequency becomes constant) no changes in the allele frequency
Equilibrium value of p is denoted with a circumflex (carrot hat): p. 
Allele frequency is changed very slowly by mutation. In the case of reversible mutation, an equilibrium state is reached where the allele frequency becomes constant.
Effect of mutationon allele frequency

Lecture 13E
Conditions for Hardy-Weinberg
Chapter 17
Dr. Neil Voss
Dec 4, 2023
Conditions for
Hardy-Weinberg
Conditions for Hardy-Weinberg Equilibrium
The Hardy-Weinberg theorem describes a hypothetical population that is not evolving
In real populations, allele and genotype frequencies do change over time
If alleles frequencies are close the Hardy-Weinberg values, we can hypothesize that the population is not evolving for that allele.
<number>
The five conditions for non-evolving populations are rarely met in nature
No mutations 
Random mating 
No natural selection 
Extremely large population size
No gene flow
When these conditions are NOT met then it implies the population is evolving.
<number>
Negative conditions
No mutations
No selective mating
No natural selection
No genetic drift
No gene flow
The Five Hardy-Weinberg Conditions for Not Evolving
Positive conditions
Alleles remain same
Random mating 
All individuals equally likely to survive
Large population size
Isolated community
For biologists studying a large flatworm population in the lab, which Hardy-Weinberg condition is most difficult to meet?
A) no selection
B) no genetic drift
C) no gene flow
D) no mutation
Answer: D
For a biologist studying a small fish population in the lab, which Hardy-Weinberg condition is easiest to meet?
A) no selection
B) no genetic drift
C) no gene flow
D) no mutation
Answer: C

Exceptions to

Nonrandom mating

Exceptions to the Hardy-Weinberg Principle
<number>
Nonrandom mating includes
Consanguineous mating (mating between genetically related individuals)
Assortative mating (mating between individuals with similar phenotypes)
Both consanguineous mating (inbreeding) and assortative mating (sexual selection) reduce the frequency of heterozygotes and increase the frequency of homozygotes compared to the Hardy-Weinberg genotype frequencies.
Nonrandom Mating
<number>
Remarkably, white birds of either sex mate almost exclusively with tan birds of the opposite sex.
A pattern which results in four effective sexes, as any given individual can breed with only one-quarter (25%) of the population
If zygotes produced by random mating have different survival rates, the genotype frequencies of the individuals that develop from these zygotes are not expected to conform to the Hardy-Weinberg predictions.
Example: Consider a randomly mating Drosophila population segregation two alleles, A1 and A2, of an autosomal gene.

Randomly matingDrosophila population
<number>
When a population is a single interbreeding unit, it is panmictic.
Panmixis implies that any member of the population is able to mate with any other member.
In nature, populations may be subdivided due to geographical or ecological barriers that may be correlated with genetic differences.
This violates the assumption that allele frequencies are uniform in the population.

for Hardy-Weinberg

Hardy-Weinberg and
Chi-square test
With the Hardy-Weinberg principle, allele frequencies can be used to predict the genotype frequencies.
For the MN blood type example,
p = 0.5395 and q = 0.4605
The predicted genotype frequencies are
LMLMp2 = (0.5395)2 = 0.2911
LMLN2pq = 2 (0.5395) (0.4605) = 0.4968
LNLNq2 = (0.4605)2 = 0.2121
Predicting Genotype Frequencies
<number>
Do these predictions fit the data?
First we must calculate the predicted genotype numbers by multiplying the Hardy-Weinberg frequencies by the sample size (6129).
GenotypePredicted Number
LMLM0.2911  6129 = 1784.2
LMLN0.4968  6129 = 3044.8
LNLN0.2121  6129 = 1300.0

X2 sum
0.0223
Next, we check for agreement between the observed and predicted numbers by calculating a chi-square statistic.
This chi-square has 3  2 = 1 degree of freedom because:
The sum of the three predicted numbers is fixed by the sample size
The allele frequency p was obtained directly from the sample data.
<number>
WAIT, why subtract 2
This chi-square has
3  2 = 1 degree of freedom because:
Subtract 1 because:
p + q = 1
Subtract 1 because:
p2 + 2pq + q2 = 1
additional restrictions on the data, remove degrees of freedom
Why 1 degree of freedom?
How many degrees of freedom does the test have?
You have to think about how many quantities are really free to vary 
remember that we used the population to estimate p and q, then we used p and q to get p2, 2pq, and q2. 
So really, once we decided on a value for p, everything else was decided for us. Whatever p was, q had to be 1-p, and p2, 2pq, and q2 were set as well. 
SO, the moral of the story is, since only p is able to vary, there is ONLY ONE degree of freedom in this chi-square test.
df = 1
H-W 2 Conclusion
Results
2 value = 0.0223
Degrees of freedom: 1
Critical value from Table: 3.841
Accept the Null Hypothesis (H0): population is NOT evolving
Conclusions:
The predicted genotype frequencies are in agreement with the observed frequencies.
In this population, the M-N genotypes are in Hardy-Weinberg proportions.

Lecture 13F
Natural Selection
Chapter 17
Dr. Neil Voss
Dec 5, 2022
2019 Update
Create worksheet where students plot out the allele frequencies over time
Natural Selection
<number>
Natural Selection
Allele frequencies change systematically in populations because of differential survival and reproduction among genotypes.
Natural selection is selection for survival and reproduction in the face of competition.
Natural selection is the mechanism that changes the physical and behavioral characteristics of a species.
<number>
Observation #1: Members of a population often vary in their inherited traits
<number>
Observation #2: All species can produce more offspring than the environment can support, and many of these offspring fail to survive and reproduce
Spore
cloud
<number>
Figure 22.UN03
Observations
Individuals in a population
vary in their heritable
characteristics.
Organisms produce more
offspring than the
environment can support.
Inferences
Individuals that are well suited
to their environment tend to leave more
offspring than other individuals.
Over time, favorable traits
accumulate in the population.
Figure 22.UN03 Summary of key concepts: natural selection
<number>
Population Evolution
Note that individuals do not evolve; populations evolve over time
Natural selection can only increase or decrease heritable traits that vary in a population
the traits must already exist in the population, unless introduced through mutation
<number>
Individuals with certain heritable traits survive and reproduce at a higher rate than other individuals (fitness)
Natural selection increases the match between organisms and their environment over time
Natural Selection:A Summary
<number>
Selection in a
Laboratory Experiment
Selection in a Lab Experiment
Bacteria are great for studying selection, because they grow fast.
A study on the competition between two genotypes A and B, under conditions in which A is the superior competitor. 
We can measure relative fitness, W as the competitive ability of of B when competing against A.
W  is called a relative fitness 
Individuals relative ability to survive and transmit genes to next generation
A = 0.6 to .995
B = 0.4 to 0.005
W = 0.96 for B
Effect of selection for a favored allele (A) in a haploid (E. coli)
Individual Fitness, w,
Relative Fitness, W, and 
Selection Coefficient
Individual Fitness, w, is the ability to survive and reproduce.
Each member of the population has its own fitness value (w):
0 if it dies or fails to reproduce,
1 if it survives and produces 1 offspring,
2 if it survives and produces 2 offspring, etc.
The average fitness of the population is calculated by averaging the fitness of individuals. 
Individual Fitness
<number>
 John Wiley & Sons, Inc.
Average Fitness and Population Size
<number>
Relative Fitness and Selection
Relative fitness, W is the contribution an individual makes to the gene pool of the next generation relative to the contributions of other individuals
Selection favors certain genotypes by acting on the phenotypes of individuals
Relative fitness, W, is calculated by taking the superior genotype (strain A) as standard fitness of 1.0
The fitness of the inferior genotypes (strainB) is expressed as a deviation from 1.
Strain B produces only 0.96 offspring for a each offspring of a strain A
^ 0.96 or 96% is the relative fitness ^
<number>
Selection Coefficient
Relative Fitness values:
Strain A has the standard fitness of 1.0
Strain B produces only 0.96 offspring for a each offspring of a strain A
Selection coefficient (s): The selective disadvantage of a disfavored genotype. 
s = 1.0  0.96 = 0.04 = 4%
The selective disadvantage of strain B is 4% per generation
The selection coefficient measures the intensity of natural selection acting on the genotypes in the population
A = 0.6 to .995
B = 0.4 to 0.005
W = 0.96 for B
s = 0.04
Effect of selection for a favored allele (A) in a haploid (E. coli)
Selection in Diploid Organisms
Dominance and recessiveness create additional complications.
Frequency of favored dominant allele changes very slowly when the allele is common.
Frequency of favored recessive allele changes very slowly when the allele is rare.
Reason: recessive alleles are found in heterozygotes, that are not exposed to selection.
remember Hardy-Weinberg and rare diseases 
Selection for a favored allele in a diploid depends upon whether the allele is dominant or recessive
Favored recessive alleles take longer to take over
True or False:
Saving lives of persons with rare recessive disorders (e.g. cystic fibrosis) will cause a deterioration of gene pool.
Rare Recessive Allelesand Selection
Implication of widely held belief: Saving lives of persons with rare recessive disorders will cause a deterioration of gene pool.
NOT TRUE reservoirs of recessive alleles is already in normal human beings who are heterozygotes.
Selection for or against a recessive alleles is very inefficient when the recessive alleles is rare.
Dark-light Insect
selection example
Natural Selection andthe Peppered Moth
The light form is homozygous for a recessive allele a, and the dark form carries a dominant allele A.
In England, after 1850, the frequency of the dark form increased dramatically
especially in industrialized areas.
<number>
Dark-Light Insects
In a particular species of insect, the Peppered Moth, fitness is determined by a single gene with two alleles, A and a.
Allele A causes insects to be dark
Allele a causes them to be light
A is completely dominant to a.
<number>
Light and Dark Formsof the Peppered Moth
<number>
In the Peppered Moth of insect, fitness is determined by a single gene with two alleles, A and a.
Allele A causes insects to be dark
Allele a causes them to be light
A is completely dominant to a.
Differential survival:
In forests, dark insects (AA and Aa) survive better
In open fields, light insects (aa) survive better.
Dark-Light Insects
<number>
Genotype
Phenotype
dark
light
Relative fitness in forest
1  s1
Relative fitness in field
1  s2
Differential survival:
In forests, dark insects (AA and Aa) survive better
In open fields, light insects (aa) survive better.
<number>
Assume that initially, p = 0.5 and q = 0.5 and that s1 = 0.1 and Hardy-Weinberg
Genotype AA
relative fitness = 1, and the frequency of AA at fertilization is p2 = 0.25.
Genotype Aa
relative fitness = 1, and the frequency of Aa at fertilization is 2pq = 0.50.
Genotype aa
relative fitness = 10.1 = 0.9, and the frequency of aa at fertilization is q2 = 0.25
Natural Selectionin the Forest Habitat
<number>
2nd Generation(Forest Habitat)
In forming the next generation, each genotype will contribute gametes in proportion to its frequency and relative fitness.
The relative contributions of the three genotypes will be
For AA, 0.25  1.0 = 0.25
For Aa, 0.50  1.0 = 0.50
For aa, 0.25  0.9 = 0.225
<number>
Normalize Proportions(Forest Habitat)
To obtain the proportional contributions of each genotype to the 2nd generation, divide their relative contributions by their sum
0.25 + 0.50 + 0.225 = 0.975
The proportional contributions to the 2nd generation are:
For AA, 0.250  0.975 = 0.256
For Aa, 0.500  0.975 = 0.513
For aa, 0.225  0.975 = 0.231
Note: 0.256+0.513+0.231 = 1.0
<number>
New Allele Frequency(Forest Habitat)
In the 2nd generation all of the alleles transmitted by aa homozygotes are a, and half the alleles transmitted by the Aa heterozygotes are a.
The frequency of a in the 2nd generation, symbolized by q' will be
q' = 0.231 +   0.513 = 0.487
This represents a loss of 2.6%
(0.5-0.487)/0.5 = 0.026
<number>
Natural Selectionin the Forest Habitat
<number>
Natural Selectionin the Field Habitat
Assume that initially,
p = 0.5 and q = 0.5 and that s2 = 0.1.
Assume that the population mates randomly and that the genotype are present in Hardy-Weinberg frequencies at fertilization in each generation.
<number>
Natural Selectionin the Field Habitat
The initial genetic composition is:
For genotype AA,
relative fitness = 10.1 = 0.9, and the frequency of AA at fertilization is p2=0.25.
For genotype Aa,
relative fitness = 10.1 = 0.9, and the frequency of Aa at fertilization is 2pq=0.50.
For genotype aa,
relative fitness = 1, and the frequency of aa at fertilization is q2=0.25
<number>
New Allele Frequency(Field Habitat)
Using the same method as before, after one generation of selection in the field habitat, the frequency of a will be
q'=0.513
In the field habitat, the frequency of recessive a alleles will increase in every generation until it equals 1
at a frequency of 1.0, it is said to be fixed in the population.
<number>
Natural Selectionin the Field Habitat
<number>
Natural Selection Comparison
Natural selection may act for or against a recessive allele.
Selection for a recessive allele and against a dominant allele is more efficient 
than selection against a recessive allele.
<number>
Directionality of
Natural Selection
Fitness can be influenced dramatically by different alleles of a single gene
but is more often influenced by many genes that affect quantitative traits such as body size, disease susceptibility, and fecundity.
fecundity  the ability to produce an abundance of offspring
Natural selection can affect the distribution of a quantitative trait through
directional selection,
disruptive selection, or 
stabilizing selection.
Natural Selection for Quantitative Traits
<number>
There are three modes of selection
Directional selection favors variants of a trait at one extreme end of its distribution
Disruptive selection favors both extreme variants of a trait at the expense of intermediate values
Stabilizing selection favors intermediate variants of a trait and acts against extreme phenotypes
Modes of Natural Selection

Selection-Mutation
Balance
Mutation-Drift Balance and Population Size
In small populations, genetic drift dominates over mutation.
In large populations, mutation dominates over genetic drift.
<number>
Selection-Mutation Balance
Selection acts to minimize the frequency of harmful alleles
but never be eliminated because mutation keeps on producing harmful alleles
So both working in opposite directions, eventually reach state of equilibrium.
<number>
S-M Equilibrium
Equilibrium depends on whether recessive allele is completely recessive or has a small effect (partial effect on the fitness of heterozygotes..)
If completely recessive, the fitness of (A is wild type and a is recessive allele):
s = selection coefficient against recessive genotypes
Selection coefficient measures the fraction of aa genotypes that fails to survive, So equilibrium value
Selection-mutation balance can be used to estimate human mutation rate.

Mutation-Drift Balance as Measured by Heterozygosity
<number>
Heterozygote
Superiority
Heterozygote Superiority
Conditions in which the heterozygote genotype has greater fitness than either of the homozygotes.
Also called overdominance
So heterozygotes produce more offspring than homozygotes
Example: Sickle cell anemia and Malaria
Heterozygote Advantage
When selection favors the heterozygotes at the expense of each type of homozygote, balancing selection, or a heterozygote advantage exists.
The relative fitness of heterozygotes is 1
and the relative fitness of the homozygotes is less than 1.
<number>
Genotype
Relative Fitness
1  s
1  t
s and t and the selection coefficients against AA and aa, respectively
In cases of heterozygote advantage, selection tends to eliminate both A and a through its effects on the homozygotes, but it also preserves those alleles through its effects on the heterozygotes.
When these opposing tendencies balance each other, a dynamic equilibrium is established.
<number>
Balanced Polymorphisms
Suppose that AA homozygotes are lethal s=1, and that the aa homozygotes are 50% as fit as the heterozygotes, t=0.5.
A dynamic equilbrium will be reached when
p = 0.5/(0.5 + 1) = 1/3 and
q = 1/(0.5 + 1) = 2/3
In this case, both alleles will be maintained at appreciable frequencies through selection in favor of the heterozygotes. 
<number>
Distribution of the diseases sickle cell anemia and falciparum malaria
The heterozygote is favored over the homozygous dominant genotype (over-dominance) in areas where malaria is prevalent.
Relative fitness's of the genotypes:
AA, AS and SS have been estimated as 0.85 : 1.00 : 0.00
therefore s = 0.15 and t = 1
p = 0.87, q = 0.13, which is close to measured values.
Selection ofSickle-Cell Alleles
Homozygotes for the S allele of the -globin gene have sickle-cell anemia.
The fitness of SS homozygotes has historically been 0.
However, in some parts of the world, notably in tropical Africa:
the frequency of the S allele is 0.2 due to moderate selection against homozygotes for A, the wild-type allele.
<number>
Sickle-Cell Anemia
AA homozygotes are more susceptible to infection by the parasites that cause malaria than SA heterozygotes.
Malaria is widespread in regions of the world where the frequency of the S allele is high.
<number>
Measuring Balancing Selection
If the equilibrium frequency of S allele is p = 0.1, and s = 1.0 because all SS homozygotes die
We can estimate the intensity of selection against the AA homozygotes due to increased susceptibility to malaria:
p = t/(s + t)
0.1 = t/(1.0 + t)
0.1(1.0 + t) = 0.1t + 0.1 = t
0.1 = (1.0  0.1)t = 0.9t
t = (0.1)/(0.9) = 0.111

Lecture 13G
Genetic Drift
Chapter 17
Dr. Neil Voss
Dec 5, 2022
Random Genetic Drift
Genetic Drift is a random process where allele frequencies change unpredictably in populations because of uncertainties during reproduction.
The smaller a sample, the greater the chance of random deviation from a predicted result
Genetic drift tends to reduce genetic variation through losses of alleles

The Founder Effect
The founder effect occurs when a few individuals become isolated from a larger population
Allele frequencies in the small founder population can be different from those in the larger parent population
<number>
The Bottleneck Effect
The bottleneck effect is a sudden reduction in population size due to a change in the environment 
The resulting gene pool may no longer be reflective of the original populations gene pool
If the population remains small, it may be further affected by genetic drift
The Bottleneck Effect

Case Study: the Greater Prairie Chicken
Loss of prairie habitat caused a severe reduction in the population of greater prairie chickens in Illinois 
The surviving birds had low levels of genetic variation, and only 50% of their eggs hatched

Scientific Response
Researchers used DNA from museum specimens to compare genetic variation in the population before and after the bottleneck
The results showed a loss of alleles at several loci
Researchers introduced greater prairie chickens from populations in other states and were successful in introducing new alleles and increasing the egg hatch rate to 90%
Heterozygosity
In large populations, the effect of genetic drift is minimal.
In small populations, genetic drift may be the primary evolutionary force.
The effect of population size is gauged by monitoring the frequency of heterozygotes, or the heterozygosity of a population over time.
Effects of Population Size
<number>
The current frequency of heterozygotes is defined as H, and the frequency of heterozygotes in the next generation is H'.
The population size is given by N.
Change in Heterozygosity
<number>
In one generation, random genetic drift causes the heterozygosity to decline by a factor of 1/2N.
In t generations, the heterozygosity, H, will decline according to the equation below until it eventually reaches 0 and all genetic variability is lost. 
<number>
Larger Populationsare Less Susceptible
<number>
Probability of AlleleFixation or Loss
If alleles are selectively neutral and the population mates randomly,
the probability that an allele will ultimately be fixed in the population is its current frequency.
the probability that it will be lost is 1 minus its current frequency.
<number>
Genetic Drift Activity
A = 0.5
a = 0.5
8 individuals
so 16 alleles
Random genetic drift in 12 hypothetical populations over 20 generations
In most of the 12 small populations (8 diploid individuals each), either the A or the a allele has become fixed.
Random genetic drift in 12 hypothetical populations over 20 generations
Overall, the frequency of the A (or a) allele has not changed when all 12 populations are looked at together
allele frequency is about constant when all populations are looked at together
Spreading out of allele frequencies means that the allele frequencies among the populations become progressively more different.
So, random genetic drift causes differences in allele frequency among subpopulations:
It is the major cause of genetic differentiation among subpopulations.
Each population consisted of 8 males and 8 females. 
The predicted and experimental results are similar except that the actual results show quite a bit more scatter.
Actual results of genetic drift in 107 experimental populations of Drosophila
bw  brown eye
bw75 - wild type
So, if random genetic drift were the only one force at work, then all alleles would be eventually become either fixed or lost
there would be no polymorphism.
But this wont happen? Why?
Summary of Genetic Drift 
Genetic drift is significant in small populations
Genetic drift can cause allele frequencies to change at random
Genetic drift can lead to a loss of genetic variation within populations
Genetic drift can cause harmful alleles to become fixed

'Incest Prevention Alarm'
You meet someone, there's chemistry, and then come the introductory questions:
What's your name? Come here often? Are you my cousin?
In Iceland, a country with a population of 320,000 where most everyone is distantly related, inadvertently kissing cousins is a real risk.
How can you tell if
an animal is inbread?
Look for the Crust!
Effects of Inbreeding
Nonrandom mating includes
Consanguineous mating  mating between genetically related individuals
Assortative mating  mating between individuals with similar phenotypes
Consanguineous mating reduces the frequency of heterozygotes and increase the frequency of homozygotes compared to the Hardy-Weinberg genotype frequencies.
Nonrandom Mating
<number>
INBREEDING
Mating between relatives such as first cousins  frequency of heterozygotes gets smaller than it is with random mating.
Start with self fertilization of Aa plant:
After first generation   AA,  Aa,  aa
it reduces proportion of heterozygous: 
After second generation:    = 1/4
After third generation:      = 1/8
So on
This section we measure the reduction in quantitative terms.
Effects of Inbreeding
Most genetic diseases are caused by recessive alleles.
Inbreeding is harmful, and much of the effect is due to rare recessive alleles that would not otherwise become homozygous.
Inbreeding increases the probability of forming recessive genotype.
Among Caucasians, albinism 1 in 20,000 among offspring of non-relatives vs. 1in 2000 among relative offspring
Blue Fugates of Kentucky
Fugates family lived in the hills of Kentucky
commonly known as the "Blue Fugates" or the Blue People
Carriers of recessive disease methemo-globinemia, which gives sufferers blue-tinged skin
Due to remote area where they lived, there was a very high level of consanguinity in marriage between families.
Inbreeding increases the chance of having progeny that are homozygous for a rare recessive trait
Effect of Inbreeding
The frequencies of the two homozygotes have increased and the frequency of the heterozygotes has decreased compared to the Hardy-Weinberg frequency.
For each genotype, the change in frequency is directly proportional to the inbreeding coefficient, F.
For a completely inbred population, F=1.
The frequency of genotype AA will be p, the frequency of Aa will be 0, and the frequency of genotype aa will be q.
<number>
The frequency of heterozygotes is reduced as inbreeding increases
Heterozygosity (H1) Graph is given by 
H1 = 2pq (1-F)
F = 0 random mating
F = 1 no heterozygous remaining in the population
Inbreeding reduces the frequency of heterozygous genotypes, relative to random mating, and increases the frequency of homozygous genotypes.
Inbreeding Coefficient
Inbreeding coefficient: It is a measure of genetic effects of inbreeding, symbolized by letter F 
Proportionate reduction in heterozygosity (H1) in an inbred organism compared with the heterozygosity expected with random mating. 
F = (2pq  H1)/2pq
F = 0 (no inbreeding)
F = 1 (complete inbreeding) ??
H1 = 2pq (1-F)
So genotypic frequencies in inbred population:
AA: p2 (1-F) + pF 
Aa: 2pq (1-F) 
aa: q2 (1-F) + qF
Inbreeding Coefficient
The effects of consanguineous mating can be quantified using the inbreeding coefficient, F.
Frequency with Genotype Consanguineous Mating
AA: p2 + pqF
Aa: 2pq  2pqF
aa: q2 + pqF
<number>
The inbreeding coefficient of individual is the probability that the individual carries alleles that are identical by descent.
Proportionate reduction in heterozygosity (H1) in an inbred organism compared with the heterozygosity expected with random mating. 
F = (2pq  H1)/2pq
F = 0 (no inbreeding)
F = 1 (complete inbreeding)
H1 = 2pq (1-F)

Calculate the
Inbreeding Coefficient
of Individual (COI)
What percent DNA do you share with your aunt?
50% offspring (mother to son)
 50% sisters (mother to aunt)
= 25% DNA shared
What percent DNA do you share with your grandfathers sisters kids?
25% grandfather
 50% sibling (gpa to sister)
 50% offspring (sister to kid)
= 0.250.50.5
= 6.25%
 2005 The Washington Post Company
DNA Shared Between Relatives
We will use the amount of shared DNA to calculate the inbreeding coefficient
My Family Story
My son was making an autobiography for the 5th grade class (during COVID).
We wanted to make a family tree for the book
The story my grandmother (born 1930) has told is that her mother had her when she was 18 and her father died when she was 2 years old.
The familysearch.org has some great tools for searching the U.S. Census, birth and death records, and grave stones.
I discovered her birth record online, which had her fathers name.
and it clearly says that my grandmother was not legitimate.
Census Record
Census Records are quite useful for finding where ancestors lived.
According to the "72-Year Rule," the National Archives releases census records to the general public 72 years after Census Day.
As a result, the 1930 census records were released April 1, 2002
The 1940 records were released April 2, 2012
The 1950 census records were newly released in April 2022.
Pedigree Collapse Calculator
Pedigree Collapse Calculator will tell you how much percentage of your DNA is homozygous indicating what level of relationship your parents had.
The tool will tell the percentage of collapse in your pedigree.
It supports FTDNA, 23andMe and Ancestry files.
The Closed Loop Method
for Calculating the
Coefficient of Inbreeding (COI)
Allelic Identity by Descent
In human genetics, geneticists wish to calculate the inbreeding coefficient of a particular individual whose pedigree is known.
The method they use is called Closed loop method
Identity by descent  the condition in which two alleles are both replicas of a single ancestral allele in the recent past (same nucleotide sequence).
If A is
not inbred, then
FA = 0
Find the Closed Loops
Two Closed Loops
Coefficient of Inbreeding
An easy way to do this calculation is by determining the number of ancestors in path.
So for Path 1 above starting at I, that would be
--> E --> C --> A --> D --> F
There are 5 ancestors in this complete path (from I back to I), and we know the probability of a particular allele being transmitted at each step is 0.5, so we can calculate the total probability as
F =          = ()5
----> or, F = 0.03125 (3.1%).
Coefficient of Inbreeding
About 3% of the genome of I will be homozygous with A.
So, for the next step, we have to consider both parents involved in this inbreeding, namely A and B.
The paths for B are just the same for A, so once again we have 5 ancester nodes, and the calculated F for B is once again 3.1%.
Finally, we compute the total probability of homozygosity in this breeding as:
 F = 3.125% + 3.125% = 6.25%;
 therefore the COI of animal/person I is 6.25%.
Complex pedigree with multiple paths
Calculation of inbreeding coefficient in a complex pedigree is more involved because each path contributes to the final inbreeding coefficient.
Complex pedigree with multiple paths
Paths:
EBD: ()3
ECD: ()3
ECABD: ()5
EBACD: ()5
FI = ()3 + ()3 + ()5 + ()5 = 5/16
Individual I is 5/16 less likely to be heterozygous than a non-inbred individual
